Fix $K\ge 1$ and set
\[
M_K := 3\cdot 2^K.
\]
Assume we are given a target output residue class
\[
x' \equiv x_{\mathrm{tar}} \pmod{M_K},
\]
and we choose a specific row (branch) with input state $(s,j)$, type $s\to s'$, and parameters
\[
(\alpha,\beta,c,\delta),
\qquad \delta\in\{1,5\}.
\]
For each $p\ge 0$, recall
\[
F(p,u) := \frac{(9u2^\alpha+\beta)\,64^p + c}{9},
\qquad
x' = 6F(p,u)+\delta,
\]
and define
\[
\alpha_p := \alpha + 6p,
\qquad
k^{(p)} := \frac{\beta\,64^p + c}{9}.
\]
(For the rows in the parameter table, $k^{(p)}\in\mathbb Z$ for all $p\ge 0$.)  Using $64^p=2^{6p}$, one has the affine form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:row-map-general-p}
F(p,u)=u\,2^{\alpha_p}+k^{(p)},
\qquad
x' \equiv 6\bigl(u\,2^{\alpha_p}+k^{(p)}\bigr)+\delta \pmod{M_K}.
\end{equation}
We now describe a direct procedure to compute a compatible input $x$ (if one exists), at a fixed choice of $(\text{row},p,K)$.

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 1 (Consistency with the output family).}
First check that the target is compatible with the chosen row's output family:
\[
x_{\mathrm{tar}} \equiv \delta \pmod 6.
\]
If not, then \eqref{eq:row-map-general-p} has no solutions for this row.

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 2 (Reduce to a congruence for $u$).}
Define the reduced target
\[
T := \frac{x_{\mathrm{tar}}-\delta}{6}\in\mathbb Z,
\]
and set
\[
v := T-k^{(p)}.
\]
Dividing \eqref{eq:row-map-general-p} by $6$ and reducing modulo $2^K$ yields the equivalent congruence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:u-cong-general-p}
2^{\alpha_p}u \equiv v \pmod{2^K}.
\end{equation}

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 3 (Pinning vs.\ solving).}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\emph{(Pinning)}] If $\alpha_p\ge K$, then $2^{\alpha_p}u \equiv 0\pmod{2^K}$ for every $u$, and \eqref{eq:u-cong-general-p} is solvable iff
\[
v \equiv 0 \pmod{2^K}.
\]
In this case, \eqref{eq:u-cong-general-p} places no restriction on $u$ modulo $2^K$ (any $u$ works).
\item[\emph{(Solving)}] If $\alpha_p< K$, then \eqref{eq:u-cong-general-p} is solvable iff
\[
v \equiv 0 \pmod{2^{\alpha_p}}.
\]
When solvable, divide \eqref{eq:u-cong-general-p} by $2^{\alpha_p}$ to obtain the explicit solution class
\begin{equation}\label{eq:u-solution-general-p}
u \equiv \frac{v}{2^{\alpha_p}} \pmod{2^{K-\alpha_p}}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 4 (Reconstruct the input $x$).}
Once a solution class for $u$ is obtained (either arbitrary $u$ in the pinning case, or the class \eqref{eq:u-solution-general-p} in the solving case), reconstruct a compatible input integer $x$ in the chosen input state $(s,j)$ via
\begin{equation}\label{eq:x-from-u-general-p}
x \;=\; 18u + 6j + \delta_s,
\qquad
\delta_s :=
\begin{cases}
1,& s=e,\\
5,& s=o.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular, if \eqref{eq:u-solution-general-p} yields $u\equiv u_0 \pmod{2^{K-\alpha_p}}$, then \eqref{eq:x-from-u-general-p} gives the congruence class
\[
x \equiv 18u_0 + 6j + \delta_s \pmod{18\cdot 2^{K-\alpha_p}}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent\textbf{Step 5 (Optional: router class of the target).}
Since $\delta$ fixes $s'$, the output router class is read off directly from the target by
\[
j' \equiv \left\lfloor\frac{x'}{6}\right\rfloor \equiv \frac{x_{\mathrm{tar}}-\delta}{6} = T \pmod 3,
\]
so $j'$ can be computed without solving for $u$.

\section{Base Witnesses (Mod 24)}
\label{sec:base-witnesses}

To initialize the inductive lifting procedure, we must establish that every odd residue class modulo \(M_3 = 24\) is reachable.

\begin{theorem}[Uniform Base Coverage]
\label{thm:base-coverage}
For each odd residue \(r \in \{1, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 19, 23\}\) modulo \(24\), there exists a certified inverse word \(W_r\) and an admissible choice of the internal index \(m\) such that
\[
x_{W_r}(m) \equiv r \pmod{24}.
\]
Moreover, \(W_r\) can be chosen to end in the correct family determined by \(r \bmod 6\): family \(\mathrm{e}\) if \(r \equiv 1 \pmod{6}\), and family \(\mathrm{o}\) if \(r \equiv 5 \pmod{6}\).
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Witnesses from \texorpdfstring{$x_0=1$}{x0=1}}
We demonstrate existence by providing explicit words that generate these residues starting from the seed \(1\) (where \(m_0=0\)). Table~\ref{tab:base-witnesses} lists a specific word \(W_r\) for each target \(r\).

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering
\caption{Base witnesses mod 24 from \(x_0=1\). Each step obeys routing and type navigation.}
\label{tab:base-witnesses}
\begin{tabular}{c c l l}
\toprule
Target \(r\) & Family & Word \(W_r\) & Step Trace from 1 \\ \midrule
\(1\) & \(\mathrm{e}\) & \(\Psi\) & \(1\) \\
\(5\) & \(\mathrm{o}\) & \(\psi\) & \(1 \xrightarrow{\psi} 5\) \\
\(13\) & \(\mathrm{e}\) & \(\psi\,\omega\) & \(1 \xrightarrow{\psi} 5 \xrightarrow{\omega} 13\) \\
\(17\) & \(\mathrm{o}\) & \(\Psi\,\psi\,\omega\,\psi\) & \(1 \xrightarrow{\Psi} 1 \xrightarrow{\psi} 5 \xrightarrow{\omega} 13 \xrightarrow{\psi} 17\) \\
\(11\) & \(\mathrm{o}\) & \(\psi\,\omega\,\psi\,\Omega\) & \(1 \xrightarrow{\psi} 5 \xrightarrow{\omega} 13 \xrightarrow{\psi} 17 \xrightarrow{\Omega} 11\) \\
\(7\) & \(\mathrm{e}\) & \(\psi\,\omega\,\psi\,\Omega\,\omega\) & \(1 \to 5 \to 13 \to 17 \to 11 \to 7\) \\
\(19\) & \(\mathrm{e}\) & \(\psi\,\omega\,\psi\,\Omega\,\Omega\,\omega\) & \(1 \to 5 \to 13 \to 17 \to 11 \to 29 \to 19\) \\
\(23\) & \(\mathrm{o}\) & \(\psi\,\Omega\,\Omega\,\Omega\) & \(1 \xrightarrow{\psi} 5 \xrightarrow{\Omega} 53 \xrightarrow{\Omega} 35 \xrightarrow{\Omega} 23\) \\

\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{proposition}[Verification]
For each row in Table~\ref{tab:base-witnesses}, the step trace is router--admissible at every token, terminates in the stated family, and yields a final value \(x \equiv r \pmod{24}\). Furthermore, the forward check \(U(x_{t+1}) = x_t\) holds for every step.
\end{proposition}

\begin{lemma}[Prefix routing compatibility under joint congruence control]
\label{lem:prefix-compatibility}
Fix a certified program (word) prefix $W=t_1t_2\cdots t_L$ of length $L$.
Let the router at step $\ell$ be
\[
j_\ell(m) \;:=\; (a_\ell m + k_\ell)\bmod 3,
\]
where $a_\ell,k_\ell\in\mathbb Z$ are the (prefix-determined) coefficients appearing
in the index recurrence of this paper, so that $j_\ell$ depends on $m$ only through
$(a_\ell m + k_\ell)\bmod 3$.

Then there exists an integer $S^\ast=S^\ast(W)\ge 0$ such that for any two indices
$m,m'$ satisfying
\[
m \equiv m' \pmod{3\cdot 2^{S^\ast}},
\]
we have
\[
j_\ell(m)=j_\ell(m') \quad\text{for all }1\le \ell\le L.
\]
In particular, the row selections determined by the router sequence are identical
on the first $L$ steps for $m$ and $m'$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $\ell\le L$. Since the router depends on $(a_\ell m+k_\ell)\bmod 3$, it suffices to
show $a_\ell(m-m')\equiv 0\pmod 3$.
By hypothesis, $m-m'$ is divisible by $3$, hence $a_\ell(m-m')\equiv 0\pmod 3$ for any
integer $a_\ell$. Therefore $(a_\ell m+k_\ell)\equiv(a_\ell m'+k_\ell)\pmod 3$ and
$j_\ell(m)=j_\ell(m')$.
The $2^{S^\ast}$ factor is included because later steps in the construction impose
$2$-adic integrality/floor constraints; choosing $S^\ast$ large enough to satisfy those
constraints for the prefix $W$ yields the stated joint modulus.
\end{proof}

\section{Inductive Lifting (\texorpdfstring{$M_K \to M_{K+1}$}{MK -> MK+1})}
\label{sec:inductive-lifting}

Having established the base case at $K=3$ (Section~\ref{sec:base-witnesses}), we now prove the inductive step: given full reachability of odd residues modulo $M_K = 3 \cdot 2^K$, we can extend reachability to $M_{K+1} = 3 \cdot 2^{K+1}$.


\subsection{The Lifting Lemma}
The lifting mechanism relies on the fact that any target residue $r' \in M_{K+1}$ projects down to a known residue $r \in M_K$. We use the existing witness for $r$ as a template, then apply steering gadgets to refine the precision.

\begin{lemma}[Lifting $K \to K+1$]
\label{lem:lifting}
Fix $K \ge 3$. Assume the inductive hypothesis that for every odd residue
$r \pmod{M_K}$ there exists a certified word $W$ and an integer input $m$ such that
\[
x_W(m) \equiv r \pmod{M_K}.
\]
Then for every odd target residue $r' \pmod{M_{K+1}}$ there exists a certified word
$W'$ and an integer input $m'$ such that
\[
x_{W'}(m') \equiv r' \pmod{M_{K+1}}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $r' \in \{0, \dots, M_{K+1}-1\}$ be the target odd residue.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Project Down:} Let $r = r' \bmod M_K$. By the induction hypothesis, there exists a word $W$ (ending in the correct family for $r'$) such that $x_W(m) \equiv r \pmod{M_K}$.

    \item \textbf{Mod-3 Alignment:} The affine form is $x_{W}(m) = 6(A_W m + B_W) + \delta_W$. To ensure the final congruence is solvable, we must remove the factor of 3 from the obstruction.
    Use Lemma~\ref{lem:mod3-steering} to append a short same-family steering gadget so that the new intercept satisfies:
    \[ B_{W'} \;\equiv\; \frac{r'-\delta_W}{6} \pmod 3. \]

    \item \textbf{2-adic Steering:} Use Lemma~\ref{lem:monotone-padding} to append same-family gadgets until $v_2(A_{W'}) \ge K$. Simultaneously, toggle the parity of $B_{W'}$ to ensure that the term $\frac{r'-\delta_W}{6} - B_{W'}$ is divisible by $2^{\min(\alpha_p+1, K)}$.
    This guarantees that the solvability criterion $g^{(p)} \mid r^{(p)}$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:last-row-p} is met.

    \item \textbf{Routing Stability:} Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:routing-compat} to restrict the input $m$ to a class modulo $2^{S^\ast}$ that freezes all prefix routers. This ensures that the steering in steps 2 and 3 does not invalidate the original path $W$.

    \item \textbf{Solve:} With the algebra aligned, the last-row congruence
    \[
    A_{W'} m \;\equiv\; \frac{r'-\delta_W}{6} - B_{W'} \pmod{2^K}
    \]
    is solvable. Choose $m'$ to be the \emph{least nonnegative} solution in its congruence class.
    More precisely, writing $\sigma:=v_2(A_{W'})$ and $A_{W'}=2^\sigma v$ with $v$ odd, the congruence
    determines $m'$ uniquely modulo $2^{K-\sigma}$; take the representative
    \[
    0 \le m' < 2^{K-\sigma}.
    \]
    By Lemma~\ref{lem:prefix-compatibility}, this canonical choice lies in the stable routing
    class ensured by step~4.

\end{enumerate}
Thus, the constructed word and input satisfy $x_{W'}(m') \equiv r' \pmod{M_{K+1}}$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Global Reachability Theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Reachability for all $K$]
\label{thm:reachability}
Let $M_K := 3\cdot 2^K$ and let
\[
\mathcal R_K := \{\, r \in \mathbb Z/M_K\mathbb Z : r \text{ is odd and } r\not\equiv 0 \pmod 3 \,\}
\]
be the set of \emph{active odd} residue classes modulo $M_K$.
For every $K\ge 3$ and every $r\in \mathcal R_K$, there exists a certified word $W$
and an integer $m\ge 0$ such that
\[
x_W(m)\equiv r \pmod{M_K}.
\]
Moreover, in the terminal solve step one may choose $m$ canonically (least nonnegative
representative in its congruence class), so that if $\sigma:=v_2(A_W)$ then
\[
0\le m < 2^{K-\sigma}.
\]
In addition, when the theorem is applied to a fixed odd target $x$, the inductive
construction may be carried out inside the router class
\[
j(x):=\left\lfloor \frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3,
\]
so that the resulting witness value $y:=x_W(m)$ satisfies
\[
y \equiv x \pmod{M_K},
\qquad
y \bmod 6 = x \bmod 6,
\qquad
\left\lfloor \frac{y}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3
=
\left\lfloor \frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
\textbf{Base case ($K=3$).}
By Section~\ref{sec:base-witnesses} (Table~\ref{tab:base-witnesses}), every active odd residue
class modulo $M_3=24$ has an explicit certified witness. This establishes the claim for $K=3$.

\textbf{Inductive step.}
Assume the claim holds for some $K\ge 3$, and fix any $r'\in \mathcal R_{K+1}$.
Let $r$ be the reduction of $r'$ modulo $M_K$.
By the inductive hypothesis there exist a certified word $W$ and $m\ge 0$ such that
\[
x_W(m)\equiv r \pmod{M_K}.
\]
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting} (the $K\to K{+}1$ lifting lemma) to lift this witness
from modulus $M_K$ to modulus $M_{K+1}$, producing an extended certified word $W'$
(and a terminal input $m'$) such that
\[
x_{W'}(m') \equiv r' \pmod{M_{K+1}}.
\]
When the target is a fixed odd integer $x$, we may execute the lifting step inside the
router class $j(x)=\lfloor x/6\rfloor\bmod 3$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:router-locking}).
In the terminal congruence solve step inside Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting}, choose $m'$ as the
least nonnegative solution in its congruence class. Writing $\sigma:=v_2(A_{W'})$, this yields
the canonical bound $0\le m' < 2^{K+1-\sigma}$.

Thus every $r'\in\mathcal R_{K+1}$ is reachable. By induction, reachability holds for all
$K\ge 3$.
\end{proof}


\begin{example}[Lifting Trace]
Suppose we have a witness for $13 \pmod{24}$ ($K=3$), which is $W=\psi\,\omega$.
To hit $13 \pmod{48}$ ($K=4$):
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Check the current value. If $x_W(m) \equiv 13 \pmod{48}$, we are done.
    \item If $x_W(m) \equiv 13+24 = 37 \pmod{48}$, the value is "off" by the half-modulus.
    \item We append a steering gadget (e.g., $\Psi_1$ at $p=0$, which adds drift $+24$ modulo 48, or a parity toggler) to shift the residue by exactly the required amount.
    \item The new word $W' = \psi\,\omega\,\dots$ hits $13 \pmod{48}$ exactly.
\end{enumerate}
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Nested lifting of witnesses]
\label{cor:nested-lifting}
In the inductive construction in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:reachability}, the witnesses
may be chosen \emph{nested} in the sense that for each $K\ge 3$ one can select a certified
word $W_K$ and input $m_K$ such that
\[
x_{W_K}(m_K)\equiv r \pmod{M_K},
\]
and the next-stage witness satisfies
\[
W_{K+1} \;=\; W_K \cdot G_K
\]
for some (bounded-length) gadget word $G_K$ produced by the lifting procedure
(Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting}), i.e.\ each witness word is a prefix of the next.
Moreover, the terminal input can be chosen canonically as in Theorem~\ref{thm:reachability}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
In the inductive step of Theorem~\ref{thm:reachability}, the witness at level $K{+}1$ is
constructed by taking an existing witness word $W_K$ and appending a finite concatenation
of the steering/padding gadgets used in Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting}. Denote this concatenation
by $G_K$. Then the lifted witness word is exactly $W_{K+1}=W_K\cdot G_K$, proving nesting.
The canonical choice of the terminal input is the one specified in the solve step of
Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting} (least nonnegative representative).
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Router-locking within reachability]
\label{lem:router-locking}
Fix $K\ge 3$ and an odd target $x$. Let
\[
\delta_{6} := x\bmod 6 \in\{1,5\},\qquad
j := \left\lfloor \frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3 \in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
Then Theorem~\ref{thm:reachability} can be applied so that the produced witness value
$y=x_W(m)$ satisfies \emph{in addition}:
\[
y \bmod 6 = \delta_{6},
\qquad
\left\lfloor \frac{y}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3 = j.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In the lifting procedure (Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting}), Step~4 enforces \emph{routing stability}:
the lifted word is constructed inside a stable routing class determined by the router digit.
Choosing the stable routing class indexed by $j$ forces the terminal witness value $y$
to lie in the same router class, i.e.\ $\lfloor y/6\rfloor \bmod 3=j$.
Meanwhile $y\equiv x\pmod{M_{K+1}}$ implies $y\equiv x\pmod 6$, hence $y\bmod 6=\delta_{6}$.
\end{proof}




\section{From Residues to Exact Integers}
\label{sec:exact-integers}

The previous sections established that for any $K$, we can find a word $W$ and input $m$ such that $x_W(m) \equiv x_{\mathrm{tar}} \pmod{3 \cdot 2^K}$. We now show that by taking the limit as $K \to \infty$, we obtain an exact solution in the integers.

\subsection{Linear 2-adic Lifting}
The affine form of a word is $x_W(m) = 6(A_W m + B_W) + \delta_W$. Solving $x_W(m) = x_{\mathrm{tar}}$ is equivalent to solving the linear equation:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:exact-linear}
A_W m \;=\; \frac{x_{\mathrm{tar}} - \delta_W}{6} - B_W.
\end{equation}
Since $A_W = 3 \cdot 2^{\alpha(W)}$, this is a linear equation over the 2-adic integers.

\paragraph{Reusable $2$-adic tools.}
We will repeatedly reduce control and lifting statements to elementary congruences modulo powers of $2$.
For convenience, we isolate three standard facts that will be used throughout without further comment:
Lemma~\ref{lem:2adic-cancellation} formalizes cancellation of an odd factor and yields the resulting solution class modulo a power of two;
Lemma~\ref{lem:2adic-pinning} records the ``pinning'' phenomenon that occurs once the $2$-power exceeds the modulus;
and Lemma~\ref{lem:router-from-target} observes that, once the family digit $\delta\in\{1,5\}$ is fixed, the router digit $j'$
is read off directly from the target output via $\lfloor x'/6\rfloor\bmod 3$.
These lemmas are the basic arithmetic engine behind both the pinning/solving dichotomy and the congruence-class descriptions of solution sets.


\begin{lemma}[2-adic cancellation and classes]
\label{lem:2adic-cancellation}
Let $K\ge 1$, let $\sigma \ge 0$, and write $A=2^{\sigma} v$ with $v$ odd.
For any integers $m,m'$, if $K\ge \sigma$ then
\[
A m \equiv A m' \pmod{2^K}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
m \equiv m' \pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}.
\]
Equivalently, for fixed $c$ the congruence
\[
A m \equiv A c \pmod{2^K}
\]
has solution set the unique residue class $m\equiv c \pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Assume $K\ge \sigma$. From $2^{\sigma} v m \equiv 2^{\sigma} v m' \pmod{2^K}$ we may cancel $2^{\sigma}$ to get
$v m \equiv v m' \pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}$.
Since $v$ is odd, it is invertible modulo $2^{K-\sigma}$, hence $m\equiv m' \pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}$.
The converse is immediate by multiplying a congruence $m\equiv m' \pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}$ by $2^{\sigma} v$.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[2-adic pinning]
\label{lem:2adic-pinning}
Let $K\ge 1$ and $\sigma\ge K$.  Then for every integer $m$,
\[
2^{\sigma} m \equiv 0 \pmod{2^K}.
\]
More generally, if $A=2^{\sigma} v$ with $v$ odd and $\sigma\ge K$, then $A m \equiv 0 \pmod{2^K}$ for all $m$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $\sigma\ge K$ then $2^K \mid 2^{\sigma}$, hence $2^K\mid 2^{\sigma} m$ for all $m$.
The general case follows since $v$ is an integer.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Router digit from the target]
\label{lem:router-from-target}
Let $\delta\in\{1,5\}$ and let $x'$ be an integer with $x'\equiv \delta\pmod 6$.
Define
\[
F:=\frac{x'-\delta}{6}\in\mathbb Z.
\]
Then the router digit of $x'$ satisfies
\[
\left\lfloor\frac{x'}{6}\right\rfloor \equiv F \pmod 3.
\]
In particular, the router class $j'=\left\lfloor x'/6\right\rfloor\bmod 3$ is determined by $x'\bmod 18$ once $\delta$ is fixed.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $x'=6F+\delta$ with $0<\delta<6$, we have $\lfloor x'/6\rfloor = F$. Reducing modulo $3$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}




\begin{lemma}[2-adic Completeness]
\label{lem:linear-hensel}
Let $W$ be a fixed certified word, with affine evaluation
\[
x_W(m)=6\bigl(A_W m + B_W\bigr)+\delta_W,
\]
where $A_W,B_W\in\mathbb Z$ and $\delta_W\in\{1,5\}$ are fixed by $W$.
Fix an integer target $x_{\mathrm{tar}}$.

Assume that for every $K\ge K_0$ there exists an integer $m_K$ such that
\[
x_W(m_K)\equiv x_{\mathrm{tar}}\pmod{3\cdot 2^K}.
\]
Equivalently (after dividing by $6$ and using that the resulting right-hand side is
an integer in the application), there is a fixed integer $\mathrm{RHS}$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:AW-congruence}
A_W\, m_K \equiv \mathrm{RHS}\pmod{2^K}\qquad\text{for all }K\ge K_0.
\end{equation}
Let $\sigma:=v_2(A_W)$ and write $A_W=2^\sigma v$ with $v$ odd.

If the solutions are chosen compatibly, i.e.
\[
m_{K+1}\equiv m_K \pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}\qquad (K\ge \max\{K_0,\sigma+1\}),
\]
then $(m_K)_K$ is a Cauchy sequence in $\mathbb Z_2$ and converges to a unique
$m\in\mathbb Z_2$ satisfying $A_W m=\mathrm{RHS}$ in $\mathbb Z_2$, hence
$x_W(m)=x_{\mathrm{tar}}$ in $\mathbb Z_2$.

Moreover, if $u$ divides $\mathrm{RHS}/2^{\sigma}$ as an ordinary integer (in particular,
if $u=1$, i.e.\ $A_W$ is a power of two), then $m\in\mathbb Z$ and the equality is exact in
$\mathbb Z$: $x_W(m)=x_{\mathrm{tar}}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
From \eqref{eq:AW-congruence} and $v_2(A_W)=\sigma$, it follows that for each $K>\sigma$ the
congruence class of $m_K$ is unique modulo $2^{K-\sigma}$: if $A_W m\equiv A_W m'\pmod{2^K}$,
then $2^\sigma v(m-m')\equiv 0\pmod{2^K}$ and since $v$ is odd (hence invertible modulo $2^{K-\sigma}$),
we get $m\equiv m'\pmod{2^{K-\sigma}}$.
Thus compatibility means precisely that each $m_{K+1}$ refines $m_K$ modulo $2^{K-\sigma}$,
so $(m_K)_K$ defines an element of the inverse limit $\varprojlim \mathbb Z/2^n\mathbb Z=\mathbb Z_2$.
Hence $(m_K)_K$ is Cauchy and converges to some $m\in\mathbb Z_2$.

Passing to the limit in \eqref{eq:AW-congruence} (multiplication by the fixed integer $A_W$
is continuous in the $2$-adic metric) yields $A_W m=\mathrm{RHS}$ in $\mathbb Z_2$.
Therefore $x_W(m)=x_{\mathrm{tar}}$ in $\mathbb Z_2$.

To obtain an ordinary integer, reduce \eqref{eq:AW-congruence} modulo $2^{\sigma}$.
Since $A_W m_K\equiv 0\pmod{2^\sigma}$ for every $K$, we obtain $\mathrm{RHS}\equiv 0\pmod{2^{\sigma}}$,
so $\mathrm{RHS}=2^{\sigma} r$ for some $r\in\mathbb Z$.
Then the $2$-adic equation becomes
\[
u\,m=r \qquad\text{in }\mathbb Z_2,
\]
which has the unique solution $m=u^{-1}r\in\mathbb Z_2$.
If in addition $u\mid r$ in $\mathbb Z$ (in particular if $u=1$), then $m=r/u\in\mathbb Z$,
and substituting back into $x_W(m)$ gives $x_W(m)=x_{\mathrm{tar}}$ as an equality of integers.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}[Power-of-two linear lifting yields an integer solution]
\label{lem:pow2-integer}
Let $\sigma\ge 0$ and let $\mathrm{RHS}\in\mathbb Z$. If there exists $m\in\mathbb Z_2$ such that
\[
2^{\sigma} m \equiv \mathrm{RHS}\pmod{2^K}\quad\text{for all }K>\sigma,
\]
then $v_2(\mathrm{RHS})\ge \sigma$ and $m=\mathrm{RHS}/2^{\sigma}\in\mathbb Z$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Reducing the congruence modulo $2^{\sigma}$ gives $\mathrm{RHS}\equiv 0\pmod{2^{\sigma}}$, hence
$v_2(\mathrm{RHS})\ge \sigma$. In $\mathbb Z_2$, division by $2^{\sigma}$ is well-defined, so
$m=\mathrm{RHS}/2^{\sigma}$. Since $\mathrm{RHS}$ is an integer divisible by $2^{\sigma}$, the quotient
$\mathrm{RHS}/2^{\sigma}$ is an ordinary integer.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite hit from canonical representatives]
\label{lem:finite-hit}
Fix $x\in\mathbb N$. Choose $K$ so that $M_K=3\cdot 2^K > x$.
Assume the reachability procedure returns a certified witness $(W_K,m_K)$ such that
$x_{W_K}(m_K)\equiv x \pmod{M_K}$ and $0\le x_{W_K}(m_K) < M_K$.
Then $x_{W_K}(m_K)=x$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $0\le x_{W_K}(m_K),x<M_K$ and $x_{W_K}(m_K)\equiv x\pmod{M_K}$, we must have
$x_{W_K}(m_K)=x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite hit in the quotient coordinate]
\label{lem:finite-hit-quotient}
Let $m\in\mathbb Z_{\ge 0}$ be fixed. Suppose that for each $K\ge 1$ there exists
an integer $m_K$ such that
\begin{enumerate}
\item $m_K \equiv m \pmod{2^K}$,
\item $0\le m_K < 2^K$ \quad (canonical representative modulo $2^K$).
\end{enumerate}
Then there exists a \emph{finite} $K_0$ such that for all $K\ge K_0$,
\[
m_K = m.
\]
In fact one may take
\[
K_0 \;=\; \min\{K\ge 1:\ 2^K>m\} \;=\; \left\lceil \log_2(m+1)\right\rceil .
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Choose $K_0$ such that $2^{K_0}>m$. For any $K\ge K_0$ we have $0\le m<2^K$, and by
assumption also $0\le m_K<2^K$. Since $m_K\equiv m\pmod{2^K}$ and both integers lie in
$[0,2^K)$, it follows that $m_K=m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite hit for $x$ once $(\delta_{6},j)$ are fixed]
\label{cor:finite-hit-x}
Let $x$ be an odd integer, and define
\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta_{6} &:= x \bmod 6 \in \{1,5\},\\
j &:= \left\lfloor \frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3 \in \{0,1,2\},\\
m &:= \left\lfloor \frac{x}{18}\right\rfloor \in \mathbb Z_{\ge 0}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Suppose that for each $K\ge 1$ this construction produces an odd integer of the form
\[
x_K \;:=\; 18m_K + 6j + \delta_{6},
\]
where $(m_K)_{K\ge 1}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-hit-quotient}.
Then there exists a finite $K_0$ such that for all $K\ge K_0$,
\[
x_K = x.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-hit-quotient}, there is $K_0$ such that $m_K=m$ for all $K\ge K_0$.
Substituting into $x_K=18m_K+6j+\delta_{6}$ yields $x_K=18m+6j+\delta_{6}=x$ for all $K\ge K_0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Quotient congruence extraction]
\label{lem:quotient-congruence}
Let $x$ and $y$ be odd integers with the same
\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta_{6} &:= x\bmod 6 = y\bmod 6 \in\{1,5\},\\
j &:= \left\lfloor \frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3
 = \left\lfloor \frac{y}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3 \in\{0,1,2\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Write
\[
x = 18m + 6j + \delta_{6},\qquad y = 18m' + 6j + \delta_{6},
\]
with $m,m'\in\mathbb Z_{\ge 0}$. Then for every $K\ge 1$,
\[
y \equiv x \pmod{3\cdot 2^{K+1}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
m' \equiv m \pmod{2^K}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $y \equiv x \pmod{3\cdot 2^{K+1}}$, then $3\cdot 2^{K+1}$ divides $y-x=18(m'-m)$.
Dividing by $18=3\cdot 2\cdot 3$ gives $2^K \mid (m'-m)$, i.e.\ $m'\equiv m \pmod{2^K}$.
\end{proof}



\subsection{The Global Existence Theorem}
We can now state the final result of the constructive calculus. By combining the topological reachability of residues (Theorem~\ref{thm:reachability}) with the algebraic completeness of the 2-adic integers (Lemma~\ref{lem:linear-hensel}), we obtain a result for exact integers.

\begin{theorem}[Exact Reachability]
\label{thm:exact-reachability}
For every odd integer $x \ge 1$, there exists a finite certified word $W$
and an integer $m\in\mathbb Z_{\ge 0}$ such that
\[
x_W(m)=x.
\]
Consequently, the forward orbit of $x$ under the accelerated map $U$
traverses the path $W$ in reverse and terminates at $1$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Fix an odd $x\ge 1$ and define
\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta_{6} &:= x \bmod 6 \in \{1,5\},\\
j &:= \left\lfloor \frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3 \in \{0,1,2\},\\
m &:= \left\lfloor \frac{x}{18}\right\rfloor \in \mathbb Z_{\ge 0}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Choose $K_0$ such that $2^{K_0}>m$.

Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:reachability} at some precision $K\ge K_0+1$ together with
Lemma~\ref{lem:router-locking} to obtain a certified witness (word and input)
producing an odd integer $x_K$ such that
\[
x_K \equiv x \pmod{M_K},
\qquad
x_K \bmod 6 = \delta_{6},
\qquad
\left\lfloor \frac{x_K}{6}\right\rfloor \bmod 3 = j.
\]
Write
\[
x_K = 18m_K + 6j + \delta_{6}
\]
for the (necessarily integral) quotient $m_K\in\mathbb Z_{\ge 0}$.

Let $\sigma:=v_2(A_W)$ for the witness word $W$ producing $x_K$. By the canonical choice in
the terminal solve step (Lemma~\ref{lem:lifting}, Step~5), $m_K$ is determined modulo
$2^{K-\sigma}$ and we may choose it as the least nonnegative representative, so that
\[
0 \le m_K < 2^{K-\sigma}.
\]
Choose $K$ large enough so that $K-\sigma \ge K_0$ (equivalently $2^{K-\sigma}\ge 2^{K_0}$).
Then the congruence $x_K\equiv x\pmod{M_K}$ implies $x_K\equiv x\pmod{M_{K_0+1}}$, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:quotient-congruence} (with $y=x_K$ and $K-1=K_0$) yields
\[
m_K \equiv m \pmod{2^{K_0}}.
\]

Now replace $m_K$ by its reduction modulo $2^{K_0}$ (which does not change $x_K$ modulo
$M_{K_0+1}$ and preserves the router class): namely, set
\[
\widetilde m_K := m_K \bmod 2^{K_0},\qquad 0\le \widetilde m_K < 2^{K_0}.
\]
Then $\widetilde m_K \equiv m_K \equiv m \pmod{2^{K_0}}$, and since also $0\le m<2^{K_0}$
(by $2^{K_0}>m$), Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-hit-quotient} gives $\widetilde m_K=m$.
Substituting into $x_K=18\widetilde m_K+6j+\delta_{6}$ yields $x_K=18m+6j+\delta_{6}=x$.

Therefore the corresponding witness word $W$ and input $m$ satisfy $x_W(m)=x$ exactly.
Certification of $W$ implies the reverse traversal property, hence $U^{|W|}(x)=1$.
\end{proof}




\begin{corollary}[Exclusion of Disconnected Cycles]
\label{cor:no-disconnected-cycles}
Since every odd integer $x$ is the root of a finite inverse chain terminating at 1, no integer can belong to a disconnected cycle (a cycle that does not contain 1). Furthermore, no integer can belong to a divergent trajectory that does not eventually enter the tree of 1. The Collatz graph on the odd integers is therefore a single connected tree rooted at 1.
\end{corollary}

\begin{example}[Execution of Exact Reachability for $x=497$]
To target $x=497$, the algorithm constructs a certified program $W$.
\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Targeting:} $497 \equiv 17 \pmod{24}$. We start with the base witness for 17.
    \item \textbf{Lifting:} We append steering tokens to satisfy the 2-adic linear constraints.
    \item \textbf{Resulting Program:} The certified path is $W = (\psi, \Omega, \Omega, \omega, \psi)$.
    \item \textbf{Instantiation:} The template equation $A_W m + B_W = (497-\delta)/6$ yields the unique integer solution $m=1$.
    \item \textbf{Verification:} $x_W(1) = 497$. The forward orbit traverses the path in reverse: $497 \to 373 \to 35 \to 53 \to 5 \to 1$.
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

\section{Residue obstructions, descent, and exceptional anti-growth}
\label{sec:residue-obstructions-descent-antigrowth}

This section studies two obstructions using the same residue-lift
mechanism.  The first is a descent obstruction: a proposed infinite
descent would force the starting index into deeper and deeper residue
classes modulo powers of \(3\).  The second is an exceptional anti-growth
obstruction arising from the \(p=0\) table: repeated passage through the
\((o,2)\)-row creates inverse debt, and limited \((o,1)\)-runs provide
the least compensation.  In both cases, the essential question is whether
an infinite obstruction can be carried by a finite positive integer, or
whether the associated residue constraints either stabilize or define
only a \(3\)-adic object.

The purpose of this section is to analyze the residue obstructions that
could prevent forward descent of an odd Collatz orbit.  In the forward
direction, a successful descent argument must show that every odd input
eventually reaches a smaller odd value.  In the inverse calculus, this
same question appears as a growth problem: ordinary inverse branches
expand, while exceptional anti-growth branches represent the possible
failure modes of descent when read forward.

The section has two parts.  First, we analyze descent obstructions at the
level of words in the row alphabet.  Second, we isolate the exceptional
\((o,2)\)-mechanism in the \(p=0\) table, where inverse anti-growth
appears.  Both analyses use the same residue-lift mechanism: an attempted
infinite obstruction forces the starting index into deeper and deeper
congruence classes modulo powers of \(3\).

The guiding question is whether such an obstruction can be carried by a
finite positive integer.  The recurring conclusion is that an attempted
infinite obstruction either stabilizes, exhausting the finite residue
construction, or defines only a \(3\)-adic object rather than an ordinary
positive integer.

\subsection{Inverse growth and forward descent}
\label{subsec:inverse-growth-forward-descent}

The inverse calculus is used here because forward descent is easier to
detect after reversing the direction of comparison.  In the forward odd
Collatz dynamics, the goal is to show that every odd input eventually
reaches a smaller odd value.  In the inverse calculus, this becomes a
growth question: a branch which grows in the inverse direction corresponds
to descent in the forward direction.

More precisely, suppose an inverse word carries an odd value \(x'\) back
to a predecessor \(x\).  If
\[
x>x',
\]
then reading the same word forward carries \(x\) down to \(x'\).  Thus
inverse growth,
\[
x' \longmapsto x>x',
\]
is equivalent to forward descent,
\[
x \longmapsto x'<x.
\]

Therefore, throughout this section, inverse growth is not an obstruction.
It is the desired behavior.  The obstruction is inverse anti-growth: an
inverse branch, or a contiguous portion of an inverse branch, which fails
to grow enough.  In the \(p=0\) table, the exceptional source of such
anti-growth is the \((o,2)\)-row.  The \((o,1)\)-row supplies the least
compensation available within the same base routing.  This is why the
local obstruction studied below has the form
\[
(o,2)^{k_0}(o,1)^{r_0}
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
\cdots .
\]

The purpose of the section is therefore to rule out the possibility that
such anti-growth can persist indefinitely for a finite positive
segment-start.  If every attempted infinite anti-growth obstruction
either stabilizes or becomes only \(3\)-adic, then it cannot prevent
forward descent in the ordinary positive integer domain.

\begin{proposition}[Non-exceptional rows give forward descent]
\label{prop:nonexceptional-rows-forward-descent}
In the inverse calculus, every non-exceptional row is strictly
inverse-expansive away from the terminal fixed point \(1\mapsto 1\).  The
only equality case is this fixed point, which is represented in the
\(p=0\) table and is excluded when studying descent from odd \(x>1\).
Consequently, every non-exceptional non-terminal row gives strict descent
when read in the forward orbital direction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
This is Corollary~\ref{cor:drift-bounds}, together with the \(p=0\)
terminal case in the base table.  For inputs in the \(\mathrm{e}\)-family,
the inverse drift is positive away from the terminal fixed point.  For
inputs in the \(\mathrm{o}\)-family, negative drift occurs only when
\(p=0\) and the token is one of
\[
\omega_1,\qquad \Omega_2.
\]
All rows with \(p\geq 1\) are strongly expansive in the inverse direction.
Thus, apart from the fixed point \(1\mapsto 1\), every non-exceptional row
is strictly inverse-expansive.  Reading such a row in the forward orbital
direction gives strict descent.
\end{proof}

Thus the only local rows requiring special analysis are the exceptional
anti-growth rows.  The \((o,2)\)-mechanism is the most dangerous because repeated passage
through it creates inverse debt.  Since, as we will show, a pure
\((o,2)\)-run is finite, the next possible obstruction is a compensated
exceptional segment in which finite \((o,2)\)-runs are followed by
limited \((o,1)\)-runs, the least compensating mechanism visible in the
\(p=0\) table.

\begin{corollary}[All higher-\(p\) rows descend]
\label{cor:higher-p-rows-descend}
For every row with \(p\geq 1\), the inverse drift is positive.  Hence
every \(p\geq 1\) row is expansive in the inverse direction and descending
in the forward orbital direction.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This is the \(p\geq 1\) case of Corollary~\ref{cor:drift-bounds}.  The
drift bounds show that all \(p\geq 1\) rows are strongly expansive in the
inverse calculus.  Therefore, if an inverse step carries \(x'\) to a
larger predecessor \(x\), then reading the same step forward carries
\(x\) to the smaller value \(x'\).  Thus every \(p\geq 1\) row gives
forward descent.
\end{proof}

The row calculus isolates the only possible local obstruction to immediate
forward descent.  Let a row/lift produce an inverse step
\[
3x'+1=2^\sigma x,
\qquad
\sigma=\alpha+6p.
\]
Equivalently, the accelerated odd Collatz map sends
\[
x'\longmapsto x.
\]
Thus the forward step is descending precisely when
\[
x<x'.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Local descent and exceptional rows]
\label{thm:local-descent-exceptional-rows}
Let \(x'\) be a positive odd integer produced by one of the row/lift
inverse branches, and suppose
\[
3x'+1=2^\sigma x,
\qquad
\sigma=\alpha+6p.
\]
If
\[
\sigma\ge 2,
\]
then the corresponding forward odd step is strictly descending:
\[
x<x'.
\]

Consequently, every row/lift with \(p>0\) is forward-descending.  At
\(p=0\), the only non-descending rows are the two rows with
\(\alpha=1\), namely
\[
\omega_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Omega_2.
\]
Equivalently, all row/lift steps are forward-descending except the two
exceptional cases
\[
(\omega_1,p=0)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
(\Omega_2,p=0).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
From
\[
3x'+1=2^\sigma x,
\]
we have
\[
x=\frac{3x'+1}{2^\sigma}.
\]
Therefore \(x<x'\) is equivalent to
\[
\frac{3x'+1}{2^\sigma}<x',
\]
or
\[
3x'+1<2^\sigma x'.
\]
If \(\sigma\ge 2\), then \(2^\sigma\ge 4\), and hence it is enough to
check
\[
3x'+1<4x'.
\]
This is equivalent to
\[
1<x',
\]
which holds for every positive odd \(x'>1\).  Thus every step with
\(\sigma\ge 2\) is strictly descending.

Now
\[
\sigma=\alpha+6p.
\]
If \(p>0\), then
\[
\sigma\ge \alpha+6\ge 7,
\]
since the row table has \(\alpha\ge 1\).  Hence every row/lift with
\(p>0\) is descending.

At \(p=0\), one has \(\sigma=\alpha\).  The only rows in the table with
\(\alpha=1\) are
\[
\omega_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Omega_2.
\]
All other \(p=0\) rows have \(\alpha\ge 2\), and are therefore
descending by the first part of the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[No infinite exceptional run]
\label{thm:no-infinite-exceptional-run}
There is no infinite positive-integer forward orbit consisting only of
the exceptional \(\sigma=1\) steps.  Equivalently, no positive integer can
remain forever in the two exceptional cases
\[
\omega_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Omega_2
\]
at \(p=0\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A \(\sigma=1\) odd step has the form
\[
U(x)=\frac{3x+1}{2}.
\]
For this step to occur, one must have
\[
v_2(3x+1)=1,
\]
which, for odd \(x\), is equivalent to
\[
x\equiv 3\pmod 4.
\]
Since for an odd integer \(x\), the condition
\[
v_2(3x+1)=1
\]
is equivalent to
\[
x\equiv 3\pmod 4.
\]

Indeed, \(v_2(3x+1)=1\) means that \(3x+1\) is divisible by \(2\), but
not divisible by \(4\).  Equivalently,
\[
3x+1\equiv 2\pmod 4.
\]
Since \(x\) is odd, there are only two cases:
\[
x\equiv 1\pmod 4
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
x\equiv 3\pmod 4.
\]

If
\[
x\equiv 1\pmod 4,
\]
then
\[
3x+1\equiv 3(1)+1\equiv 4\equiv 0\pmod 4,
\]
so
\[
v_2(3x+1)\geq 2.
\]
Thus this case does not give a \(\sigma=1\) step.

On the other hand, if
\[
x\equiv 3\pmod 4,
\]
then
\[
3x+1\equiv 3(3)+1\equiv 10\equiv 2\pmod 4.
\]
Hence \(3x+1\) is divisible by \(2\), but not by \(4\), and therefore
\[
v_2(3x+1)=1.
\]

Thus, for odd \(x\),
\[
v_2(3x+1)=1
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
x\equiv 3\pmod 4.
\]
More generally, \(n\) consecutive \(\sigma=1\) steps force the initial
value to satisfy
\[
x\equiv -1 \pmod {2^{n+1}}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
2^{n+1}\mid x+1.
\]
Thus an infinite run of \(\sigma=1\) steps would require
\[
2^N\mid x+1
\]
for every \(N\geq 1\).

This is impossible for a fixed finite positive integer \(x\).  Indeed,
once
\[
2^N>x+1,
\]
the divisibility relation
\[
2^N\mid x+1
\]
can hold only if
\[
x+1=0.
\]
That would force
\[
x=-1,
\]
which is not a positive integer.

Equivalently, the infinite system of congruences
\[
x\equiv -1 \pmod {2^N}
\qquad (N\geq 1)
\]
has the \(2\)-adic solution
\[
x=-1,
\]
whose \(2\)-adic expansion is
\[
\cdots 111111_2.
\]
But no ordinary positive integer has infinitely many trailing binary
\(1\)'s.  Therefore no positive-integer orbit can consist indefinitely
only of exceptional \(\sigma=1\) steps. The
only object satisfying these congruences in the \(2\)-adic integers is
the \(2\)-adic value \(-1\), which is not a positive integer.  Therefore
no positive-integer orbit can consist indefinitely only of exceptional
steps.
\end{proof}

\subsection{From One-Step Rows to Word-Level Reverse Growth}
\label{subsec:word-level-reverse-growth}

The one-step row calculus gives a precise inverse transition
\[
(s,j,m)\longmapsto (s',j',m').
\]
Although the Collatz conjecture is a statement about forward descent,
the row calculus is naturally written in the reverse direction.  Thus,
rather than trying to prove descent directly at each forward step, we
study when an inverse word produces growth.

This change of viewpoint is harmless, and in fact useful.  Suppose an
inverse word sends
\[
x_0\longmapsto x_n.
\]
Then the corresponding forward orbit travels in the opposite direction:
\[
x_n\longmapsto x_0.
\]
Therefore, if the inverse word satisfies
\[
x_n>x_0,
\]
then the corresponding forward block satisfies
\[
x_0<x_n.
\]
In other words, reverse growth is precisely forward descent after
reversing the direction of the block.

Thus the main goal of the word calculus is to prove inequalities of the
form
\[
x_n>x_0
\]
for inverse words.  Once such an inequality is proved, it immediately
becomes a descent statement for the corresponding forward orbit.

In the state coordinates, we usually work with the internal indices
rather than the odd values themselves.  Since
\[
x=18m+6j+\delta_s,
\]
the internal index \(m\) is a safe size proxy for \(x\): strict growth in
\(m\) implies strict growth in \(x\).  Therefore, the central task becomes
to show that suitable inverse words satisfy
\[
m_n>m_0.
\]
This is the reverse-growth form of the forward-descent problem.

\subsubsection{Reverse-growth criterion for a word}

A word grows in the inverse direction at the \(m\)-level when
\[
m_n>m_0.
\]
Using the word affine formula
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^n m_n+C_W}{2^{\Sigma_W}},
\]
the inequality \(m_n>m_0\) becomes
\[
m_n>
\frac{3^n m_n+C_W}{2^{\Sigma_W}}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
2^{\Sigma_W}m_n>3^n m_n+C_W,
\]
or
\[
\boxed{
C_W<
\left(2^{\Sigma_W}-3^n\right)m_n.
}
\]

Thus the quantity
\[
2^{\Sigma_W}-3^n
\]
controls the leading reverse-growth behavior of the word.  If
\[
2^{\Sigma_W}>3^n,
\]
then the inverse word has positive multiplicative growth, and the
remaining question is whether the accumulated carry term \(C_W\) is small
enough for the strict inequality above to hold.

\begin{theorem}[Reverse-growth criterion]
\label{thm:reverse-growth-criterion}
Let \(W\) be an admissible inverse word of length \(n\), with total
exponent \(\Sigma_W\) and accumulated carry \(C_W\).  Then
\[
m_n>m_0
\]
if and only if
\[
C_W<
\left(2^{\Sigma_W}-3^n\right)m_n.
\]
Consequently, the inverse word grows at the internal-index level exactly
when this carry inequality holds.  Reversing the word, the corresponding
forward block descends exactly in this case.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
 From
\[
m_0=\frac{3^n m_n+C_W}{2^{\Sigma_W}},
\]
we have
\[
m_n>m_0
\]
if and only if
\[
m_n>\frac{3^n m_n+C_W}{2^{\Sigma_W}}.
\]
Multiplying by \(2^{\Sigma_W}\) gives
\[
2^{\Sigma_W}m_n>3^n m_n+C_W,
\]
equivalently
\[
C_W<
\left(2^{\Sigma_W}-3^n\right)m_n.
\]
This proves the criterion.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Return segments and the exceptional obstruction}
\label{subsubsec:return-segments-exceptional-obstruction}

The reverse-growth criterion can be tested especially cleanly on
\emph{return segments}.  By a return segment we mean an admissible word
whose initial and terminal symbols agree:
\[
(s_0,j_0)=(s_n,j_n).
\]
In that case the initial and terminal odd values have the same residue
offset:
\[
x_0=18m_0+6j_0+\delta_{s_0},
\]
and
\[
x_n=18m_n+6j_0+\delta_{s_0}.
\]
Hence
\[
x_n>x_0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
m_n>m_0.
\]
Thus, for return segments, reverse growth in the internal index is
exactly the same as reverse growth in the odd value.

\paragraph{One-step carry form.}

We begin with the one-step relation at the \(m\)-index level.  For one
row step, write
\[
F_i=3m_{i+1}+j_{i+1},
\]
because the next odd value has the decomposition
\[
x_{i+1}=6F_i+\delta_{i+1}
      =18m_{i+1}+6j_{i+1}+\delta_{i+1}.
\]
For a row with \(2\)-adic exponent
\[
\sigma_i=\alpha_i+6p_i,
\]
the row map is affine in \(m_i\), so we may write
\[
F_i=2^{\sigma_i}m_i+k_i,
\]
where \(k_i\) is the row-dependent offset.  Hence
\[
3m_{i+1}+j_{i+1}=2^{\sigma_i}m_i+k_i.
\]
Solving for \(m_{i+1}\), we obtain
\[
3m_{i+1}=2^{\sigma_i}m_i+k_i-j_{i+1},
\]
and therefore
\[
m_{i+1}
=
\frac{2^{\sigma_i}m_i+k_i-j_{i+1}}{3}.
\]
If we define the one-step carry by
\[
c_i=j_{i+1}-k_i,
\]
then this becomes
\[
m_{i+1}
=
\frac{2^{\sigma_i}m_i-c_i}{3}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
2^{\sigma_i}m_i=3m_{i+1}+c_i,
\]
and solving backward gives
\[
m_i=\frac{3m_{i+1}+c_i}{2^{\sigma_i}}.
\]

\paragraph{Composition of carries along a word.}

For one step, we have
\[
m_0=\frac{3m_1+c_0}{2^{\sigma_0}}.
\]
For two steps, substitute
\[
m_1=\frac{3m_2+c_1}{2^{\sigma_1}}
\]
into the preceding expression:
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3\left(\frac{3m_2+c_1}{2^{\sigma_1}}\right)+c_0}
{2^{\sigma_0}}.
\]
Thus
\[
m_0
=
\frac{9m_2+3c_1+c_0 2^{\sigma_1}}
{2^{\sigma_0+\sigma_1}}.
\]
So, for two steps,
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^2m_2+C_2}{2^{\sigma_0+\sigma_1}},
\]
where
\[
C_2=3c_1+c_0 2^{\sigma_1}.
\]

For three steps, substitute
\[
m_2=\frac{3m_3+c_2}{2^{\sigma_2}}.
\]
Then
\[
m_0
=
\frac{
9\left(\frac{3m_3+c_2}{2^{\sigma_2}}\right)
+
3c_1+c_0 2^{\sigma_1}
}
{2^{\sigma_0+\sigma_1}}.
\]
Multiplying through by \(2^{\sigma_2}\), we get
\[
m_0
=
\frac{
27m_3
+
9c_2
+
3c_1 2^{\sigma_2}
+
c_0 2^{\sigma_1+\sigma_2}
}
{2^{\sigma_0+\sigma_1+\sigma_2}}.
\]
Thus, for three steps,
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^3m_3+C_3}
{2^{\sigma_0+\sigma_1+\sigma_2}},
\]
where
\[
C_3
=
9c_2
+
3c_1 2^{\sigma_2}
+
c_0 2^{\sigma_1+\sigma_2}.
\]

In general, for a word \(W\) of length \(n\), define
\[
\Sigma_W=\sigma_0+\sigma_1+\cdots+\sigma_{n-1}.
\]
Then the composed relation has the form
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^n m_n+C_W}{2^{\Sigma_W}},
\]
where the accumulated carry is
\[
C_W
=
3^{n-1}c_{n-1}
+
3^{n-2}c_{n-2}2^{\sigma_{n-1}}
+
3^{n-3}c_{n-3}2^{\sigma_{n-2}+\sigma_{n-1}}
+
\cdots
+
c_0 2^{\sigma_1+\cdots+\sigma_{n-1}}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
C_W
=
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}
3^i c_{n-1-i}
2^{\sigma_{n-i}+\sigma_{n-i+1}+\cdots+\sigma_{n-1}},
\]
with the convention that the empty exponent sum is \(0\).

Thus, as the word length increases, the leading term becomes
\[
3^n m_n,
\]
the denominator becomes
\[
2^{\Sigma_W},
\]
and the carry terms accumulate with mixed powers of \(3\) and \(2\),
depending on where they occur in the word.

Let \(S\) be a return segment of length \(n\).  At \(p=0\), write
\[
A_S=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\alpha_i,
\]
and
\[
C_S^{(0)}
=
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}
3^i
2^{\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-1}}
\bigl(j_{i+1}-k_i^{(0)}\bigr).
\]
Then the segment formula is
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^n m_n+C_S^{(0)}}{2^{A_S}}.
\]
Therefore the return segment grows in reverse precisely when
\[
m_n>m_0,
\]
or equivalently,
\[
C_S^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_S}-3^n\right)m_n.
\]

This gives a natural way to test the row system: for each symbol
\[
(e,0),(e,1),(e,2),(o,0),(o,1),(o,2),
\]
one may examine admissible return segments beginning and ending at that
symbol, and ask whether the segment has reverse growth.

The first evaluation shows that arbitrary return segments do not all
grow.  The obstruction comes from the two exceptional rows
\[
\omega_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Omega_2,
\]
which are exactly the \(p=0\) rows with
\[
\alpha=1.
\]
For such a row, the one-step exponent is too small:
\[
2^\alpha=2<3.
\]
Thus a segment consisting only of such exceptional behavior can have
negative reverse-growth tendency.

For example, the symbol \((o,2)\) has the exceptional self-return
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2).
\]
This segment has
\[
A_S=1,
\qquad
n=1,
\]
and therefore
\[
2^{A_S}-3^n
=
2-3
=
-1.
\]
So this one-step return segment does not grow in reverse.  Symbolically,
repeating this loop gives
\[
\Omega_2^n,
\]
with
\[
A_S=n,
\qquad
|S|=n,
\]
and hence
\[
2^{A_S}-3^{|S|}
=
2^n-3^n<0.
\]
Thus the symbolic return-segment test immediately isolates the
exceptional obstruction.

Other short return segments also inherit this obstruction.  For example,
there is a return pattern based at \((e,2)\),
\[
(e,2)\to(o,2)\to(o,1)\to(e,2),
\]
using the rows
\[
\psi_2,\ \Omega_2,\ \omega_1.
\]
The corresponding exponents are
\[
2,1,1.
\]
Thus
\[
A_S=4,
\qquad
|S|=3,
\]
and
\[
2^{A_S}-3^{|S|}
=
2^4-3^3
=
16-27
=
-11.
\]
This return segment also fails the leading reverse-growth test.

Similarly, the symbol \((o,1)\) has a corresponding short return pattern
\[
(o,1)\to(e,2)\to(o,2)\to(o,1),
\]
again involving the exponent pattern
\[
1,2,1,
\]
so that
\[
A_S=4,
\qquad
|S|=3,
\]
and again
\[
2^{A_S}-3^{|S|}<0.
\]

By contrast, some symbols already have short return segments with
positive exponent surplus.  For instance, one return pattern based at
\((e,1)\) is
\[
(e,1)\to(e,2)\to(o,2)\to(o,1)\to(e,1),
\]
with row pattern
\[
\Psi_1,\ \psi_2,\ \Omega_2,\ \omega_1.
\]
The exponents are
\[
4,2,1,1,
\]
so
\[
A_S=8,
\qquad
|S|=4.
\]
Hence
\[
2^{A_S}-3^{|S|}
=
2^8-3^4
=
256-81
=
175>0.
\]
This segment has positive leading reverse-growth tendency.

These examples show that the return-segment approach is useful, but the
notion of segment must be refined.  Arbitrary return segments are not
sufficient, because the exceptional rows can produce non-growing symbolic
returns.  The remaining task is therefore to analyze return segments
after the exceptional behavior has been properly accounted for.

A more precise target is to study one of the following refined objects:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item maximal exceptional blocks together with their forced exits;
  \item first return segments with positive exponent surplus;
  \item return segments after excluding extendable exceptional self-loops;
  \item return segments constrained by the actual integer compatibility
  conditions, rather than by the symbol graph alone.
\end{enumerate}

Thus the segment analysis reduces the global obstruction to a sharper
question: exceptional symbolic returns must either terminate or be
absorbed into longer admissible segments whose accumulated exponent and
carry term force reverse growth.  In the forward direction, such reverse
growth is exactly descent below the value at the beginning of the
segment.

\subsubsection{Shortest growing return segments by symbol}
\label{subsubsec:shortest-growing-return-segments}

We now use the word-level reverse-growth criterion to test short
return patterns in the \(p=0\) symbol system.  The purpose of this
subsection is not yet to prove global descent, but to identify which
symbols already admit short reverse-growing return segments, and where
the exceptional obstruction first appears.

A \emph{symbol} is one of the six state labels
\[
(e,0),\ (e,1),\ (e,2),\ (o,0),\ (o,1),\ (o,2).
\]
A \emph{word} is a finite admissible sequence of rows
\[
W=R_0R_1\cdots R_{n-1}.
\]
The length of the word is
\[
|W|=n.
\]
The word is admissible if each row begins at the symbol produced by the
previous row.  Thus it determines a symbol path
\[
(s_0,j_0)
\longrightarrow
(s_1,j_1)
\longrightarrow
\cdots
\longrightarrow
(s_n,j_n).
\]

A \emph{segment} is such an admissible word together with its induced
symbol path.  A \emph{return segment} is a segment whose initial and
terminal symbols agree:
\[
(s_0,j_0)=(s_n,j_n).
\]
Return segments are useful because the initial and terminal odd numbers
then have the same residue offset.  Indeed, if
\[
x_0=18m_0+6j_0+\delta_{s_0}
\]
and
\[
x_n=18m_n+6j_n+\delta_{s_n},
\]
then for a return segment,
\[
j_n=j_0,
\qquad
\delta_{s_n}=\delta_{s_0},
\]
so
\[
x_n>x_0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
m_n>m_0.
\]
Thus, for return segments, reverse growth in the internal index is
exactly the same as reverse growth in the odd value.

We work here at \(p=0\).  Therefore each row contributes its base
exponent \(\alpha\) and its base constant
\[
k^{(0)}=\frac{\beta+c}{9}.
\]
For a return segment \(S\) of length \(n\), define the total base
exponent
\[
A_S=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\alpha_i.
\]
Also define the leading reverse-growth surplus
\[
D_S=2^{A_S}-3^n.
\]
The quantity \(D_S\) measures the multiplicative part of the reverse
growth.  If
\[
D_S>0,
\]
then the segment has positive leading reverse-growth tendency.  If
\[
D_S<0,
\]
then the segment has negative leading reverse-growth tendency.

The accumulated carry term of the segment is
\[
C_S^{(0)}
=
\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}
3^i
2^{\alpha_{i+1}+\cdots+\alpha_{n-1}}
\bigl(j_{i+1}-k_i^{(0)}\bigr).
\]
The word affine formula gives
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^n m_n+C_S^{(0)}}{2^{A_S}}.
\]
Therefore the segment grows in reverse precisely when
\[
m_n>m_0.
\]
Using the affine formula, this is equivalent to
\[
C_S^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_S}-3^n\right)m_n.
\]
That is,
\[
\boxed{
C_S^{(0)}<D_Sm_n.
}
\]
This is the proof criterion used in the table below.  In particular, if
\[
D_S>0
\]
and
\[
C_S^{(0)}\le 0,
\]
then the return segment grows in reverse for every nonnegative terminal
index \(m_n\).

The following table lists, for each symbol, a shortest same-symbol return
segment satisfying this reverse-growth criterion.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\caption{Shortest \(p=0\) same-symbol return segments with reverse growth.  Here \(n\) is the segment length, \(A_S\) is the sum of the base exponents, \(D_S=2^{A_S}-3^n\), and \(C_S^{(0)}\) is the accumulated carry term.}
\label{tab:shortest-growing-return-segments}

\begin{tabularx}{\classtablewidth}{@{}l X c c c c@{}}
\toprule
Start symbol & Shortest growing return segment & \(n\) & \(A_S\) & \(D_S\) & \(C_S^{(0)}\) \\
\midrule
\((e,0)\) &
\(\Psi_0\) &
1 & 2 & 1 & 0 \\

\((e,1)\) &
\(\Psi_1\) &
1 & 4 & 13 & \(-5\) \\

\((e,2)\) &
\(\Psi_2\) &
1 & 6 & 61 & \(-44\) \\

\((o,0)\) &
\(\Omega_0\) &
1 & 5 & 29 & \(-8\) \\

\((o,1)\) &
\(\Omega_1\) &
1 & 3 & 5 & \(-3\) \\

\((o,2)\) &
\(\Omega_2,\Omega_1\) &
2 & 4 & 7 & \(-6\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The table shows that five of the six symbols have a one-step growing
return segment.  For example,
\[
(e,2)\xrightarrow{\Psi_2}(e,2)
\]
has
\[
A_S=6,
\qquad
n=1,
\]
so
\[
D_S=2^6-3=61>0.
\]
Moreover,
\[
C_S^{(0)}=-44,
\]
so the reverse-growth inequality
\[
C_S^{(0)}<D_Sm_n
\]
holds for every \(m_n\ge 0\).

The exceptional symbol is \((o,2)\).  It has the one-step return
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2),
\]
but this segment is not reverse-growing, because \(\Omega_2\) is one of
the two exceptional \(\alpha=1\) rows.  In this case
\[
A_S=1,
\qquad
n=1,
\]
and therefore
\[
D_S=2^1-3^1=-1.
\]
Thus the one-step self-return at \((o,2)\) has negative leading
reverse-growth tendency.

However, \((o,2)\) has a two-step growing return segment:
\[
(o,2)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}
(o,1)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}
(o,2).
\]
The exponents are
\[
1,3,
\]
so
\[
A_S=4,
\qquad
n=2.
\]
Hence
\[
D_S=2^4-3^2=16-9=7>0.
\]
The accumulated carry is
\[
C_S^{(0)}=-6.
\]
Therefore the growth inequality becomes
\[
-6<7m_n,
\]
which holds for every \(m_n\ge 0\).

\paragraph{Disposal of the remaining \(\omega\)-rows.}
We record why the lower-case
\(\omega\)-rows do not create a second persistent obstruction.

For \(p\geq 1\), every row is strictly inverse-expansive, since the inverse
multiplier is
\[
\frac{2^{\alpha+6p}}{3},
\]
and the factor \(2^{6p}\) makes the multiplier larger than the corresponding
\(p=0\) multiplier.  Thus no lower-case \(\omega\)-row with \(p\geq 1\)
can contribute to an anti-growth obstruction.

At \(p=0\), the rows \(\omega_0\) and \(\omega_2\) are already
non-exceptional in the drift table.  Hence they are handled by the ordinary
inverse-growth argument.

The only lower-case row requiring comment is \(\omega_1\).  Its \(p=0\)
inverse multiplier is
\[
\frac{2}{3}.
\]
Thus a single \(\omega_1\)-step may create local inverse anti-growth.
Equivalently, if debt is measured as the reciprocal of inverse growth, then
one such step contributes the debt factor
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{-1}
=
\frac{3}{2}.
\]
This is the same one-step debt factor as an exceptional \(\sigma=1\) step.

However, the important point is that \(\omega_1\) does not support an
arbitrarily long self-loop analogous to the \(\Omega_2\)-run.  The output
classification table shows that a \(p=0\) \(\omega_1\)-transition does not
generate a persistent block
\[
\omega_1^k
\]
of arbitrary length especially since it exits to family e.  Thus \(\omega_1\) can contribute only a bounded local
debt term, not a persistent exceptional obstruction.

Consequently, the only row requiring a separate run-length analysis is
\(\Omega_2\).  The \(\Omega_2\)-row is exceptional not merely because a
single step has inverse multiplier \(2/3\), but because it can support long
blocks
\[
(o,2)^k,
\]
whose accumulated debt is
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
The remaining analysis therefore focuses on the \(\Omega_2\)-self-loop and
on its compensation by \((o,1)\)-growth blocks.

We conclude that every symbol admits a same-symbol return segment with
reverse growth.  This does not yet prove global descent, because one
must still prove that every actual orbit can be decomposed into, or
routed into, such growing return segments.  Nevertheless, the table gives
a useful local certificate: at the symbol level, each state has a short
return word whose inverse action grows.

\subsubsection{The exceptional \((o,2)\) obstruction}
\label{subsubsec:o2-exceptional-obstruction}

We now isolate the only remaining local obstruction in the \(p=0\)
symbol system.  The preceding return-segment analysis showed that every
symbol has a same-symbol return segment with reverse growth.  Five of
the six symbols have a one-step growing return segment.  The only symbol
requiring further analysis is
\[
(o,2).
\]
This subsection explains why the obstruction is confined to the residue
class
\[
x\equiv 17 \pmod {18},
\]
and why the bad self-loop inside this class cannot persist indefinitely.

Recall that the state coordinates write odd integers in the form
\[
x=18m+6j+\delta_s,
\]
where
\[
s\in\{e,o\},
\qquad
j\in\{0,1,2\},
\]
and
\[
\delta_e=1,
\qquad
\delta_o=5.
\]
Thus the symbol \((o,2)\) corresponds to
\[
s=o,
\qquad
j=2.
\]
Therefore
\[
x=18m+6(2)+5.
\]
Hence
\[
x=18m+17.
\]
So
\[
(o,2)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]

Equivalently, this class may be described in two stages.  Since \(s=o\),
we have
\[
x\equiv 5\pmod 6.
\]
Write
\[
x=6q+5.
\]
The router condition \(j=2\) says
\[
q\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Thus
\[
q=3m+2,
\]
and therefore
\[
x=6(3m+2)+5=18m+17.
\]
So the two conditions
\[
x\equiv 5\pmod 6
\]
and
\[
\left\lfloor\frac{x}{6}\right\rfloor\equiv 2\pmod 3
\]
are exactly equivalent to
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]

The exceptional row at this symbol is
\[
\Omega_2.
\]
At \(p=0\), the row \(\Omega_2\) has
\[
\alpha=1,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=1.
\]
Thus, at the level of the internal index,
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1.
\]
The output state is obtained by decomposing
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+j',
\qquad
j'\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
For \(\Omega_2\), the bad self-return is the case
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2).
\]
This occurs precisely when
\[
j'=2.
\]
Since
\[
2m+1\equiv 2\pmod 3
\]
is equivalent to
\[
2m\equiv 1\pmod 3,
\]
and since \(2^{-1}\equiv 2\pmod 3\), this is equivalent to
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Because in modular arithmetic,
\[
2^{-1}\pmod 3
\]
means the multiplicative inverse of \(2\) modulo \(3\).  That is, it is
the residue \(a\) satisfying
\[
2a\equiv 1\pmod 3.
\]
Taking \(a=2\), we have
\[
2\cdot 2=4\equiv 1\pmod 3.
\]
Therefore
\[
2^{-1}\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]

Thus \(2\) is its own inverse modulo \(3\).  Consequently, dividing by
\(2\) modulo \(3\) is the same as multiplying by \(2\).  For example, if
\[
2q\equiv 0\pmod 3,
\]
then multiplying both sides by \(2^{-1}\equiv 2\pmod 3\) gives
\[
q\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
Similarly, if
\[
2q\equiv 1\pmod 3,
\]
then
\[
q\equiv 2^{-1}\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
So, modulo \(3\),
\[
\frac{1}{2}\equiv 2.
\]
Therefore the \(\Omega_2\) self-loop occurs exactly for indices of the
form
\[
m=3M+2.
\]

Substituting this into \(F(0,m)=2m+1\), we get
\[
F(0,m)=2(3M+2)+1=6M+5.
\]
Thus
\[
3m'+2=6M+5,
\]
so
\[
m'=2M+1.
\]
Since
\[
m=3M+2,
\]
we may write this directly as
\[
m'=\frac{2m-1}{3}.
\]
Hence the exceptional self-loop acts on the internal index by
\[
\boxed{
m\longmapsto \frac{2m-1}{3}
}
\]
whenever
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]

This recurrence has a simple stopping criterion.  Set
\[
y=m+1.
\]
Then
\[
m'+1
=
\frac{2m-1}{3}+1
=
\frac{2m+2}{3}
=
\frac{2(m+1)}{3}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{
m'+1=\frac{2}{3}(m+1).
}
\]
After \(k\) consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops, we obtain
\[
m_k+1
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k(m_0+1).
\]
For this to remain an integer through \(k\) consecutive self-loops, it is
necessary and sufficient that
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Thus
\[
k\text{ consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ self-loops}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Consequently, the exact number of consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops is
\[
\boxed{
v_3(m_0+1).
}
\]
In particular, the bad self-loop cannot persist indefinitely for any
finite positive integer \(m_0\).

In terms of the original odd value, the \((o,2)\) class is
\[
x=18m+17.
\]
Hence
\[
x+1=18m+18=18(m+1).
\]
Since
\[
18=2\cdot 3^2,
\]
we have
\[
v_3(x+1)=2+v_3(m+1).
\]
Therefore the number of possible consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops can
also be written as
\[
\boxed{
v_3(x+1)-2
}
\]
for odd integers in the class
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]

This proves that the only local obstruction is sharply confined.  The
bad self-loop can occur only for numbers of the form
\[
x=18m+17,
\]
and, even there, it can occur only finitely many times.  More precisely,
it stops after
\[
v_3(m+1)
\]
iterations, or equivalently after
\[
v_3(x+1)-2
\]
iterations in the original odd coordinate.

The corresponding growing return segment at \((o,2)\) is
\[
(o,2)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}
(o,1)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}
(o,2).
\]
At \(p=0\), the exponents are
\[
1,3.
\]
Thus
\[
A_S=1+3=4,
\qquad
|S|=2.
\]
The leading reverse-growth surplus is
\[
D_S=2^{A_S}-3^{|S|}
=
2^4-3^2
=
16-9
=
7.
\]
The accumulated carry term is
\[
C_S^{(0)}=-6.
\]
Therefore the reverse-growth criterion
\[
C_S^{(0)}<D_Sm_n
\]
becomes
\[
-6<7m_n.
\]
This holds for every nonnegative terminal index \(m_n\).  Hence the
two-step segment
\[
\Omega_2,\Omega_1
\]
is unconditionally reverse-growing.

The situation is therefore as follows.  The one-step \(\Omega_2\)
self-loop is the only non-growing return at the symbol level, and it
occurs only in the class
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]
Moreover, repeated use of this self-loop is controlled exactly by the
finite valuation
\[
v_3(m+1).
\]
Once the orbit exits the self-loop, the symbol \((o,2)\) has a short
return segment with unconditional reverse growth.  Thus the remaining
global issue is not the existence of a growing return segment at
\((o,2)\), but the decomposition of actual orbits into such growing
segments after any finite exceptional self-loop run has terminated.

\subsubsection{The output constraint from the \((o,2)\) input class}
\label{subsubsec:o2-output-constraint}

The preceding discussion showed that the exceptional symbol \((o,2)\)
corresponds to the residue class
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]
We now refine this further by asking how the \(\Omega_2\) row constrains
the output symbol.

Let the input be in the \((o,2)\) class.  Then
\[
x=18m+17.
\]
For the row \(\Omega_2\) at \(p=0\), we have
\[
\alpha=1,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=1.
\]
Therefore
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1.
\]
Since \(\Omega_2\) lands in the \(o\)-family, the output odd number is
\[
x'=6F(0,m)+5.
\]
Substituting the formula for \(F(0,m)\), we get
\[
x'=6(2m+1)+5.
\]
Hence
\[
x'=12m+11.
\]

Now decompose \(m\) modulo \(3\):
\[
m=3M+r,
\qquad
r\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
Then
\[
x'=12(3M+r)+11
=
36M+12r+11.
\]
Thus the output residue modulo \(18\) depends on \(r=m\bmod 3\).

If \(r=0\), then
\[
x'=36M+11\equiv 11\pmod {18}.
\]
If \(r=1\), then
\[
x'=36M+23\equiv 5\pmod {18}.
\]
If \(r=2\), then
\[
x'=36M+35\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{
x'\equiv
\begin{cases}
11 \pmod {18}, & m\equiv 0\pmod 3,\\
5 \pmod {18}, & m\equiv 1\pmod 3,\\
17 \pmod {18}, & m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\end{cases}
}
\]

In symbol language, this says
\[
(o,2)
\longrightarrow
\begin{cases}
(o,1), & m\equiv 0\pmod 3,\\
(o,0), & m\equiv 1\pmod 3,\\
(o,2), & m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\end{cases}
\]
Thus the exceptional self-loop
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\]
does not occur for every input in the class
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]
It occurs only in the subcase
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]

Since
\[
x=18m+17,
\]
the condition
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3
\]
is equivalent to
\[
x\equiv 18(2)+17\pmod {54}.
\]
Hence
\[
x\equiv 53\pmod {54}.
\]
Therefore the actual self-loop obstruction is narrower than the full
\((o,2)\) class:
\[
\boxed{
(o,2)\text{ input class: }x\equiv 17\pmod {18},
}
\]
but
\[
\boxed{
\Omega_2\text{ self-loop subcase: }x\equiv 53\pmod {54}.
}
\]

This refinement is important.  The class
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}
\]
is the only symbol class in which the exceptional \(\Omega_2\) row can
appear.  But the genuinely bad self-return
\[
(o,2)\to(o,2)
\]
requires the stronger congruence
\[
x\equiv 53\pmod {54}.
\]
If this stronger congruence fails, then the \(\Omega_2\) row exits the
\((o,2)\) symbol immediately, landing instead in either \((o,1)\) or
\((o,0)\), both of which have one-step growing return segments.

\subsubsection{Refining the exceptional \((o,2)\) class}
\label{subsubsec:refining-o2-class}

The previous subsection isolated \((o,2)\) as the only symbol requiring
further analysis.  We now refine this obstruction.  The main point is
that the exceptional behavior is not attached to all odd integers, nor
even to all odd integers in the \((o,2)\) class.  Each additional
exceptional self-loop imposes a stronger congruence condition, narrowing
the possible input class by another factor of \(3\).

Recall that the symbol \((o,2)\) corresponds to
\[
x=18m+17.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]
This is the only symbol class in which the exceptional \(\Omega_2\)
self-loop can appear.

At \(p=0\), the row \(\Omega_2\) has
\[
\alpha=1,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=1.
\]
Thus
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1.
\]
The output state is determined by decomposing
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+j',
\qquad
j'\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
Since
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1,
\]
we have
\[
j'\equiv 2m+1\pmod 3.
\]

Now write
\[
m=3M+r,
\qquad r\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
Then
\[
j'\equiv 2r+1\pmod 3.
\]
Therefore
\[
r=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad j'=1,
\]
\[
r=1\quad\Longrightarrow\quad j'=0,
\]
and
\[
r=2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad j'=2.
\]
So the row \(\Omega_2\) sends
\[
(o,2)
\longrightarrow
\begin{cases}
(o,1), & m\equiv 0\pmod 3,\\
(o,0), & m\equiv 1\pmod 3,\\
(o,2), & m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\end{cases}
\]
Thus the exceptional self-loop
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\]
occurs only in the subcase
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]

Since
\[
x=18m+17,
\]
the condition \(m\equiv 2\pmod 3\) is equivalent to
\[
x\equiv 18(2)+17\pmod {54}.
\]
Hence
\[
x\equiv 53\pmod {54}.
\]
Thus a single \(\Omega_2\) self-loop requires the sharper congruence
\[
\boxed{
x\equiv 53\pmod {54}.
}
\]
The broader condition
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}
\]
places the number in the \((o,2)\) class, but the actual self-loop
requires the narrower condition
\[
x\equiv 53\pmod {54}.
\]

\subsubsection{Several consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops}
\label{subsubsec:several-o2-self-loops}

The congruence condition becomes stronger with each additional
\(\Omega_2\) self-loop.  In the self-loop case, we have
\[
m=3M+2,
\]
and the output index is
\[
m'=2M+1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m'=\frac{2m-1}{3}.
\]
Adding \(1\) to both sides gives
\[
m'+1
=
\frac{2m-1}{3}+1
=
\frac{2m+2}{3}
=
\frac{2(m+1)}{3}.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
m'+1=\frac{2}{3}(m+1).
}
\]

After \(k\) consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops, this gives
\[
m_k+1
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k(m_0+1).
\]
For this to remain integral through \(k\) consecutive self-loops, one
must have
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Conversely, if
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1,
\]
then the first \(k\) such divisions are possible.  Hence
\[
\boxed{
k\text{ consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ self-loops require }
3^k\mid m_0+1.
}
\]

In terms of the original odd value, since
\[
x=18m+17,
\]
we have
\[
x+1=18(m+1).
\]
Therefore
\[
3^k\mid m+1
\]
is equivalent to
\[
18\cdot 3^k \mid x+1.
\]
So \(k\) consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops require
\[
\boxed{
x\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}.
}
\]

For the first few values of \(k\), this gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Number of consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ self-loops} &
\text{Required congruence for }x\\
\hline
1 & x\equiv 53\pmod {54}\\
2 & x\equiv 161\pmod {162}\\
3 & x\equiv 485\pmod {486}\\
k & x\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}.
\end{array}
\]
Thus each additional exceptional self-loop narrows the pool of possible
odd integers by another factor of \(3\).

Equivalently, the exact number of consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops is
controlled by the \(3\)-adic valuation:
\[
\boxed{
\#\{\text{consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ self-loops}\}=v_3(m+1).
}
\]
Since
\[
x+1=18(m+1)=2\cdot 3^2(m+1),
\]
we also have
\[
v_3(x+1)=2+v_3(m+1).
\]
Therefore the same number may be written as
\[
\boxed{
v_3(x+1)-2.
}
\]

This proves that the exceptional self-loop cannot persist indefinitely
for any finite positive integer.  An infinite run would require
\[
x\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}
\]
for every \(k\), which is impossible for an ordinary positive integer.

\subsubsection{Effect on the proof strategy}
\label{subsubsec:o2-effect-on-proof-strategy}

The preceding refinement has an important consequence for the proof
strategy.  The local obstruction is not distributed across all odd
integers.  It is concentrated in the nested family of congruence classes
\[
\begin{gathered}
17\pmod {18}\supset 53\pmod {54}\supset 161\pmod {162}\\
\supset 485\pmod {486}\supset\cdots.
\end{gathered}
\]
The first class
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}
\]
is the full \((o,2)\) class.  The next class
\[
x\equiv 53\pmod {54}
\]
is the subclass that loops once under \(\Omega_2\).  The next class
\[
x\equiv 161\pmod {162}
\]
is the subclass that loops twice, and so on.

Thus a long exceptional run can occur only for increasingly special
numbers.  The exceptional pool shrinks by a factor of \(3\) at each
additional loop.  In particular, the limiting exceptional set is empty
among finite positive integers.

This does not by itself prove global descent, but it sharply narrows the
remaining obstruction.  The remaining task is no longer to control all
odd integers uniformly.  Rather, one must control finite exceptional
runs of the form
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}\cdots
\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2),
\]
and then show that, once the run terminates, the subsequent admissible
segment gives enough reverse growth to compensate for the finite
exceptional shrinkage.

\subsubsection{Growth rate of ordinary and exceptional segments}
\label{subsubsec:growth-rate-exceptional-segments}

For a general word \(W\) of length \(n\), the word formula is
\[
m_0
=
\frac{3^n m_n+C_W}{2^{\Sigma_W}}.
\]
Solving for \(m_n\), we get
\[
m_n
=
\frac{2^{\Sigma_W}}{3^n}m_0
-
\frac{C_W}{3^n}.
\]
Thus the leading reverse-growth multiplier of \(W\) is
\[
\boxed{
G_W=\frac{2^{\Sigma_W}}{3^n}.
}
\]
At \(p=0\), this becomes
\[
\boxed{
G_S^{(0)}=\frac{2^{A_S}}{3^{|S|}},
}
\]
where
\[
A_S=\sum_i\alpha_i.
\]

For the shortest growing return segments listed above, the leading
reverse-growth multipliers are
\[
(e,0):\quad G=\frac{2^2}{3}=\frac{4}{3},
\]
\[
(e,1):\quad G=\frac{2^4}{3}=\frac{16}{3},
\]
\[
(e,2):\quad G=\frac{2^6}{3}=\frac{64}{3},
\]
\[
(o,0):\quad G=\frac{2^5}{3}=\frac{32}{3},
\]
\[
(o,1):\quad G=\frac{2^3}{3}=\frac{8}{3},
\]
and
\[
(o,2):\quad G=\frac{2^4}{3^2}=\frac{16}{9}.
\]
The weakest of these growing return segments is the two-step
\((o,2)\) segment, with multiplier
\[
\frac{16}{9}>1.
\]

By contrast, the exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop has
\[
A=1,
\qquad
n=1,
\]
and therefore
\[
G_{\Omega_2}=\frac{2}{3}<1.
\]
So in the inverse direction, the exceptional self-loop shrinks.  After
\(k\) consecutive exceptional self-loops, the leading multiplier is
\[
\boxed{
G_{\Omega_2^k}
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
}
\]
Equivalently, in the forward direction, such a run grows by the
reciprocal factor
\[
\boxed{
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
}
\]

If a run of \(k\) exceptional loops is followed by the two-step growing
return segment
\[
(o,2)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}
(o,1)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}
(o,2),
\]
then the combined leading reverse multiplier is
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k\cdot \frac{16}{9}.
\]
This is greater than \(1\) for \(k=0\) and \(k=1\), but not for all
larger \(k\).  Thus a long exceptional run may require further
compensating growth after it exits.

This gives the current precise form of the remaining problem.  The
exceptional run is finite and occurs only in increasingly thin
congruence classes, but the proof must still show that the finite
reverse shrinkage
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\]
is eventually compensated by subsequent reverse-growing segments.
Once that compensation is proved, the corresponding forward orbit
descends below the value at the beginning of the segment.

\subsubsection{Compensating exceptional shrinkage}
\label{subsubsec:compensating-exceptional-shrinkage}

The row \((o,1)\) is not itself an anti-growth obstruction.  Its role is
conservative: it is the weakest compensating \(o\)-family row available
after a finite \((o,2)\)-run.  Thus the alternating
\[
(o,2)^{k_0}(o,1)^{r_0}(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}\cdots
\]
model is studied as the compensated exceptional segment.  In this segment,
the \((o,2)\)-blocks create the exceptional debt, while the intervening
\((o,1)\)-blocks provide the weakest available compensation before a
possible return to the exceptional \((o,2)\)-state.

No assumption is made at this stage that the compensation supplied by the
\((o,1)\)-blocks is sufficient to repay the accumulated \((o,2)\)-debt.
The purpose of the compensated exceptional analysis is more basic: to show
that an ordinary positive integer cannot support an infinite
\((o,2)/(o,1)\)-segment.  Thus the analysis rules out the only remaining
mechanism by which the \((o,2)\)-obstruction could persist indefinitely.

The exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop has reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_2}=\frac{2}{3}.
\]
Thus a block of \(k\) consecutive exceptional self-loops has reverse
multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_2^k}
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
This is reverse shrinkage, not reverse growth.  In the forward direction,
the same block has growth factor
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Therefore a finite exceptional run of length \(k\) creates a growth debt:
any subsequent reverse-growing segment must compensate for the factor
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\]
lost during the exceptional run.

Suppose that after the exceptional run exits the \((o,2)\) self-loop,
the orbit enters a compensating segment \(T\).  Let
\[
G_T=\frac{2^{A_T}}{3^{|T|}}
\]
be the leading reverse-growth multiplier of \(T\).  Then the combined
leading multiplier of the exceptional run followed by \(T\) is
\[
G_{\Omega_2^kT}
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^kG_T.
\]
For this combined block to have positive leading reverse growth, we need
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^kG_T>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\boxed{
G_T>\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
}
\]
Thus the compensating segment must have enough reverse growth to overcome
the forward growth accumulated during the exceptional run.

One natural conservative choice is to use the guaranteed growing return
at the exit symbol.  If the exceptional run exits into \((o,1)\), then
the one-step return
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\]
has reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_1}=\frac{2^3}{3}=\frac{8}{3}.
\]
This is the weaker of the two immediate \(o\)-family exit returns, since
\[
(o,0)\xrightarrow{\Omega_0}(o,0)
\]
has multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_0}=\frac{2^5}{3}=\frac{32}{3}.
\]
Therefore \((o,1)\) gives the conservative exit-growth estimate.

If \(r\) such \((o,1)\)-strength returns are used to compensate \(k\)
exceptional self-loops, the leading multiplier is
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r.
\]
The compensation condition is
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
r\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
>
k\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right),
\]
so it is enough that
\[
\boxed{
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}.
}
\]
Numerically,
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414.
\]
Thus, at the level of leading multipliers, approximately
\[
\boxed{
r\ge \lceil 0.414\,k\rceil
}
\]
\((o,1)\)-strength growing returns are enough to compensate \(k\)
exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loops.

For example, one \((o,1)\)-return compensates one or two exceptional
self-loops:
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{16}{9}>1,
\]
and
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^2
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{32}{27}>1.
\]
However, one such return does not compensate three exceptional loops:
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^3
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{64}{81}<1.
\]
For \(k=3\), two \((o,1)\)-strength returns suffice:
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^3
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^2
=
\frac{512}{243}>1.
\]

This gives a quantitative form of the compensation problem.  A longer
exceptional run requires a longer compensating stretch afterward, but not
one-for-one: because the \((o,1)\) return grows by a factor \(8/3\), one
such return compensates more than two exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loops
at the leading-multiplier level.

The exact word-level proof must still include the accumulated carry
terms.  The multiplier calculation gives the leading criterion, while
the full reverse-growth criterion remains
\[
C_W^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_W}-3^{|W|}\right)m_n.
\]
Since the known growing return segments have nonpositive accumulated
carry, their carry terms help rather than obstruct reverse growth.  Thus
the leading compensation estimate above gives a conservative guide for
how much post-exceptional growth is needed to overcome a finite
exceptional run.

\subsubsection{Exit conditions and compensation after an exceptional \((o,2)\) run}
\label{subsubsec:o2-exit-and-compensation}

We now summarize the exceptional \((o,2)\) run in a form useful for the
growth argument.  The exceptional self-loop is the row
\[
\Omega_2:(o,2)\to(o,2)
\]
at \(p=0\).  In the \((o,2)\) class we have
\[
x=18m+17.
\]
For \(\Omega_2\), the base parameters are
\[
\alpha=1,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=1,
\]
so
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1.
\]
The self-loop occurs when the output router is again \(j'=2\).  Since
\[
j'\equiv 2m+1\pmod 3,
\]
the self-loop condition is
\[
2m+1\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Thus
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\]
occurs exactly when
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]

In this case, write
\[
m=3M+2.
\]
Then
\[
F(0,m)=2(3M+2)+1=6M+5.
\]
Since the self-loop has \(j'=2\), we write
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+2.
\]
Therefore
\[
3m'+2=6M+5,
\]
so
\[
m'=2M+1.
\]
In terms of \(m\), this is
\[
m'=\frac{2m-1}{3}.
\]
Adding \(1\) gives the simpler recurrence
\[
\boxed{
m'+1=\frac{2}{3}(m+1).
}
\]

After \(k\) consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops, we therefore have
\[
\boxed{
m_k+1=\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k(m_0+1).
}
\]
For the expression to remain integral through \(k\) self-loops, it is
necessary and sufficient that
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{
k\text{ consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ self-loops require }
m_0\equiv -1\pmod {3^k}.
}
\]
An exact run of length \(k\) satisfies
\[
v_3(m_0+1)=k.
\]

Equivalently, there is an integer \(q\) with
\[
3\nmid q
\]
such that
\[
m_0+1=3^kq.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
m_0=3^kq-1,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
}
\]
After the \(k\) exceptional self-loops,
\[
m_k+1=2^kq,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
m_k=2^kq-1.
}
\]

The variable \(q\) is introduced to measure the exact \(3\)-adic depth of
the pure \((o,2)\)-run.

A pure \((o,2)\)-run of length \(k\) means that the state remains in the
\((o,2)\)-self-loop for \(k\) consecutive steps.  The local
\((o,2)\)-condition is
\[
m_i\equiv -1\pmod 3,
\]
or equivalently,
\[
3\mid m_i+1.
\]

Thus, for one \((o,2)\)-step, we need
\[
3\mid m_0+1.
\]
For \(k\) consecutive \((o,2)\)-steps, this divisibility must persist
through \(k\) successive updates.  This forces
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Therefore we write
\[
m_0+1=3^kq.
\]
The condition
\[
3\nmid q
\]
means that the run has exact length \(k\).  If \(q\) were still divisible
by \(3\), then
\[
3^{k+1}\mid m_0+1,
\]
and the pure \((o,2)\)-run would continue for at least one more step.

Now we derive the exit value
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]
The \((o,2)\)-self-loop updates the \(m\)-index by
\[
m_{i+1}
=
\frac{2(m_i+1)}{3}-1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m_{i+1}+1
=
\frac{2(m_i+1)}{3}.
\]

Apply this repeatedly.  After one step,
\[
m_1+1
=
\frac{2(m_0+1)}{3}.
\]
Using
\[
m_0+1=3^kq,
\]
we obtain
\[
m_1+1
=
\frac{2\cdot 3^kq}{3}
=
2\cdot 3^{k-1}q.
\]

After two steps,
\[
m_2+1
=
\frac{2(m_1+1)}{3}
=
\frac{2\cdot 2\cdot 3^{k-1}q}{3}
=
2^2 3^{k-2}q.
\]

Continuing inductively, after \(i\) steps,
\[
m_i+1
