=
2^i3^{k-i}q.
\]
Therefore, after \(k\) steps,
\[
m_k+1
=
2^k3^{k-k}q
=
2^kq.
\]
Hence
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]

Thus each \((o,2)\)-step removes one factor of \(3\) from \(m_i+1\) and
introduces one factor of \(2\).  After \(k\) such steps, all \(k\) factors
of \(3\) have been removed and replaced by \(k\) factors of \(2\):
\[
3^kq
\quad\longmapsto\quad
2^kq.
\]
This is the reason for the formula
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]


In terms of the odd value
\[
x_0=18m_0+17,
\]
the condition becomes
\[
x_0+1=18(m_0+1)=18\cdot 3^kq.
\]
Therefore \(k\) consecutive exceptional self-loops require
\[
\boxed{
x_0\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}.
}
\]
For the first few values this gives
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Number of consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ loops} &
\text{Required congruence for }x_0\\
\hline
1 & x_0\equiv 53\pmod {54}\\
2 & x_0\equiv 161\pmod {162}\\
3 & x_0\equiv 485\pmod {486}\\
k & x_0\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}.
\end{array}
\]
Thus each additional exceptional loop narrows the possible input class by
another factor of \(3\).

\paragraph{Examples of exact exceptional runs.}

For an exact \(k\)-loop example, the simplest choice is
\[
q=1,
\]
so
\[
m_0=3^k-1.
\]
Then
\[
m_i+1=2^i3^{k-i},
\]
and hence
\[
\boxed{
m_i=2^i3^{k-i}-1.
}
\]
The corresponding odd values are
\[
x_i=18m_i+17
=
18(2^i3^{k-i}-1)+17,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
x_i=18\cdot 2^i3^{k-i}-1.
}
\]

For \(k=1\),
\[
m_0=3^1-1=2.
\]
The exceptional segment is
\[
2\longmapsto 1.
\]
In odd values,
\[
x_0=18(2)+17=53,
\qquad
x_1=18(1)+17=35.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
53\longmapsto 35.
}
\]

For \(k=5\),
\[
m_0=3^5-1=242.
\]
The exceptional segment is
\[
242\longmapsto 161\longmapsto 107\longmapsto 71
\longmapsto 47\longmapsto 31.
\]
In odd values this is
\[
4373\longmapsto 2915\longmapsto 1943\longmapsto 1295
\longmapsto 863\longmapsto 575.
\]

For \(k=7\),
\[
m_0=3^7-1=2186.
\]
The exceptional segment is
\[
2186
\longmapsto
1457
\longmapsto
971
\longmapsto
647
\longmapsto
431
\longmapsto
287
\longmapsto
191
\longmapsto
127.
\]
In odd values this is
\[
\begin{gathered}
39365\longmapsto 26243\longmapsto 17495\longmapsto 11663\\
\longmapsto 7775\longmapsto 5183\longmapsto 3455\longmapsto 2303.
\end{gathered}
\]

These examples all use \(q=1\).  In general, the terminal residue after
an exact \(k\)-loop run is
\[
m_k\equiv 2^kq-1\pmod 3.
\]
Since \(3\nmid q\), this terminal residue is either \(0\) or \(1\), but
never \(2\).  Therefore the exceptional run must exit the \((o,2)\)
self-loop.  More precisely,
\[
m_k\equiv 0\pmod 3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(o,2)\to(o,1),
\]
whereas
\[
m_k\equiv 1\pmod 3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(o,2)\to(o,0).
\]
Thus every finite exceptional run exits to either \((o,1)\) or \((o,0)\).

\paragraph{Worst-case exit compensation.}

Both exit symbols have unconditional same-symbol growing returns.  The
\((o,0)\) return
\[
(o,0)\xrightarrow{\Omega_0}(o,0)
\]
has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_0}=\frac{2^5}{3}=\frac{32}{3}.
\]
The \((o,1)\) return
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\]
has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_1}=\frac{2^3}{3}=\frac{8}{3}.
\]
Since
\[
\frac{8}{3}<\frac{32}{3},
\]
the \((o,1)\) exit gives the conservative, worst-case compensation
estimate.

The exceptional block \(\Omega_2^k\) has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_2^k}
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
This is reverse shrinkage.  In the forward direction, it corresponds to
growth by
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
If \(r\) \((o,1)\)-strength growing returns are used to compensate \(k\)
exceptional loops, the combined leading reverse multiplier is
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r.
\]
For this to exceed \(1\), we need
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
r\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
>
k\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right).
\]
Therefore it is enough that
\[
\boxed{
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}.
}
\]
Numerically,
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414.
\]
Thus, at the leading-multiplier level,
\[
\boxed{
r\ge \lceil 0.414\,k\rceil
}
\]
\((o,1)\)-strength growing returns compensate \(k\) exceptional
\((o,2)\) self-loops.

Equivalently, fewer than half as many \((o,1)\)-strength growing returns
are needed to compensate a run of exceptional \((o,2)\) loops.  For
example,
\[
k=2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r=1\text{ is enough},
\]
since
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^2
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{32}{27}>1.
\]
For \(k=3\), one return is not enough:
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^3
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{64}{81}<1,
\]
but two returns are enough:
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^3
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^2
=
\frac{512}{243}>1.
\]
Similarly,
\[
k=5
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r=3\text{ is enough},
\]
and
\[
k=10
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r=5\text{ is enough}.
\]

This gives a quantitative compensation principle.  A long exceptional
\((o,2)\) run creates reverse shrinkage by the factor
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k,
\]
but the conservative \((o,1)\) exit-growth multiplier
\[
\frac{8}{3}
\]
is strong enough that approximately \(41.4\%\) as many such growing
returns compensate the loss at the leading-multiplier level.  The exact
word-level verification must still include the carry term, using
\[
C_W^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_W}-3^{|W|}\right)m_n,
\]
but the known \((o,1)\) and \((o,0)\) returns have nonpositive carry, so
their carry contributions assist rather than obstruct reverse growth.

\begin{lemma}[Exit from a finite \(\Omega_2\) run]
\label{lem:exit-from-finite-omega2-run}
Let an orbit remain in the exceptional self-loop
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\]
for exactly \(k\ge 1\) consecutive steps.  Then, after the \(k\)-th
self-loop, the next \(\Omega_2\)-transition cannot return to \((o,2)\).
Instead, it exits to either \((o,0)\) or \((o,1)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
An exact \(k\)-step self-loop run satisfies
\[
m_0+1=3^k q,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
\]
After \(k\) self-loops,
\[
m_k+1=2^k q,
\]
so
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]
Since \(3\nmid q\), the residue \(m_k\bmod 3\) is either \(0\) or \(1\),
but not \(2\).  The self-loop \((o,2)\to(o,2)\) occurs exactly when
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Therefore the next \(\Omega_2\)-transition cannot be another self-loop.
For \(\Omega_2\), the routing is
\[
m\equiv 0\pmod 3 \Longrightarrow (o,2)\to(o,1),
\]
and
\[
m\equiv 1\pmod 3 \Longrightarrow (o,2)\to(o,0).
\]
Hence the exact finite run exits to either \((o,1)\) or \((o,0)\).
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{No infinite positive \(\Omega_2\) self-loop}
\label{subsubsec:no-infinite-positive-omega2-loop}

We now record the finiteness of the exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop.
This is the key point that prevents the exceptional \((o,2)\) behavior
from persisting forever in the positive integers.

Recall that the exceptional self-loop is
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2).
\]
At \(p=0\), the row \(\Omega_2\) has
\[
\alpha=1,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=1,
\]
so
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1.
\]
The self-loop occurs precisely when
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
In that case, writing
\[
m=3M+2,
\]
we get
\[
F(0,m)=2(3M+2)+1=6M+5.
\]
Since the output router is again \(j'=2\), we write
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+2.
\]
Thus
\[
3m'+2=6M+5,
\]
and hence
\[
m'=2M+1.
\]
In terms of \(m\), this is
\[
m'=\frac{2m-1}{3}.
\]
Adding \(1\) to both sides gives the simpler recurrence
\[
\boxed{
m'+1=\frac{2}{3}(m+1).
}
\]

Therefore, after \(k\) consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops,
\[
\boxed{
m_k+1=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k(m_0+1).
}
\]
For the first \(k\) self-loops to be possible in the ordinary integers,
one must have
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m_0\equiv -1\pmod {3^k}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[No infinite positive \(\Omega_2\) self-loop]
\label{lem:no-infinite-positive-omega2-loop}
No positive integer state can remain forever in the exceptional
\(\Omega_2\) self-loop
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2).
\]
Equivalently, every positive-integer \(\Omega_2\) self-loop run is
finite.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose, for contradiction, that a positive integer state remains in the
\(\Omega_2\) self-loop forever.  Then for every \(k\ge 1\), the first
\(k\) self-loops are possible.  Hence
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1
\]
for every \(k\ge 1\).

The only ordinary integer divisible by every power of \(3\) is \(0\).
Therefore
\[
m_0+1=0,
\]
so
\[
m_0=-1.
\]
But \(m_0=-1\) does not correspond to a positive \((o,2)\) state.  Indeed,
in the \((o,2)\) class,
\[
x_0=18m_0+17,
\]
so
\[
x_0=18(-1)+17=-1.
\]
This is not a positive integer.  Hence no positive integer can remain in
the \(\Omega_2\) self-loop indefinitely.
\end{proof}

Thus an infinite exceptional run is possible only as the formal fixed
point
\[
m=-1,
\qquad
x=-1,
\]
outside the positive Collatz domain.  Indeed,
\[
\frac{2(-1)-1}{3}=-1.
\]
So \(-1\) is the non-positive limiting object associated with the
exceptional self-loop, but it is not a positive integer state.

For every positive integer in the \((o,2)\) class, the exceptional run
therefore terminates after finitely many steps.  More precisely, if
\[
v_3(m_0+1)=k,
\]
then the self-loop occurs exactly \(k\) times.  After those \(k\) loops,
we may write
\[
m_0+1=3^kq,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
\]
Then
\[
m_k+1=2^kq,
\]
so
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]
Since \(3\nmid q\), we cannot have
\[
m_k\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Indeed, if
\[
m_k\equiv 2\pmod 3,
\]
then
\[
2^kq-1\equiv 2\pmod 3,
\]
so
\[
2^kq\equiv 0\pmod 3,
\]
forcing
\[
q\equiv 0\pmod 3,
\]
a contradiction.

Hence
\[
m_k\equiv 0\pmod 3
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
m_k\equiv 1\pmod 3.
\]
Under the \(\Omega_2\) routing,
\[
m_k\equiv 0\pmod 3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(o,2)\to(o,1),
\]
and
\[
m_k\equiv 1\pmod 3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(o,2)\to(o,0).
\]
Therefore every finite positive \(\Omega_2\) self-loop run exits to one
of the two good symbols:
\[
\boxed{
(o,0)\quad\text{or}\quad(o,1).
}
\]

Combining these observations, the exceptional obstruction has the
following exact form:
\[
\boxed{
\text{A positive }(o,2)\text{ state may loop under }\Omega_2
\text{ only finitely many times,}
}
\]
and after the finite run terminates, it must exit to either \((o,0)\) or
\((o,1)\).  Thus the exceptional self-loop cannot produce an infinite
positive-integer shrink path to \(1\), to \(0\), or to \(-\infty\);
within the positive-integer system, the infinite exceptional path does
not exist.

\begin{lemma}[Logarithmic bound on exceptional \((o,2)\) runs]
\label{lem:logarithmic-bound-o2-runs}
Let
\[
x_0=18m_0+17
\]
be a positive odd integer in the \((o,2)\) class.  Suppose that the
\(\Omega_2\) self-loop
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\]
occurs for \(k\) consecutive steps.  Then
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Consequently,
\[
k\le \left\lfloor \log_3(m_0+1)\right\rfloor.
\]
Equivalently, since
\[
m_0+1=\frac{x_0+1}{18},
\]
we have
\[
\boxed{
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{x_0+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor.
}
\]
Thus the length of an exceptional \((o,2)\) run is bounded
logarithmically in the starting value.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop at \(p=0\), the internal
index satisfies
\[
m_{i+1}+1=\frac{2}{3}(m_i+1).
\]
After \(k\) consecutive self-loops, this gives
\[
m_k+1=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k(m_0+1).
\]
For \(m_k\) to be an integer after \(k\) such steps, it is necessary that
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Therefore
\[
3^k\le m_0+1.
\]
Taking logarithms base \(3\), we get
\[
k\le \log_3(m_0+1).
\]
Since \(k\) is an integer,
\[
k\le \left\lfloor \log_3(m_0+1)\right\rfloor.
\]

Finally, because the \((o,2)\) class has
\[
x_0=18m_0+17,
\]
we have
\[
x_0+1=18(m_0+1),
\]
and hence
\[
m_0+1=\frac{x_0+1}{18}.
\]
Substituting this into the previous bound gives
\[
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{x_0+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor.
\]
\end{proof}

The bound is sharp in the following sense.  For any \(k\ge 1\), choose
\[
m_0=3^k-1.
\]
Then
\[
m_0+1=3^k,
\]
so
\[
v_3(m_0+1)=k.
\]
Thus this choice gives an exact \(k\)-step exceptional \((o,2)\) run.
The corresponding odd value is
\[
x_0=18(3^k-1)+17=18\cdot 3^k-1.
\]
Therefore there is no universal finite maximum length for exceptional
\((o,2)\) runs over all positive integers.  However, for any fixed size
bound \(x_0\le X\), the length satisfies
\[
\boxed{
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{X+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor.
}
\]

\paragraph{Numerical size bounds.}

The logarithmic bound also shows that very large numerical ranges can
contain only modest-length exceptional runs.  If
\[
x_0\le X,
\]
then
\[
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{X+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor.
\]
For example,
\[
X=10^6
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{10^6+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor
=
9.
\]
Similarly,
\[
X=10^{12}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{10^{12}+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor
=
22,
\]
and
\[
X=10^{18}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k\le
\left\lfloor
\log_3\left(\frac{10^{18}+1}{18}\right)
\right\rfloor
=
35.
\]
Thus even below \(10^{18}\), an exceptional \((o,2)\) run can have length
at most \(35\).  The possible length grows only logarithmically with the
starting value.
So exceptional runs can be arbitrarily long in principle, but their
length grows only logarithmically with the starting value.

\subsubsection{A growth-debt budget for exceptional runs}
\label{subsubsec:growth-debt-budget-exceptional-runs}

The exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop creates reverse shrinkage.  A run
of \(k\) consecutive exceptional loops has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_2^k}
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
Equivalently, in the forward direction, the same block has growth factor
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
It is therefore useful to regard a \(k\)-step exceptional run as creating
a \emph{growth debt}
\[
\boxed{
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
}
\]
This debt measures the amount of subsequent reverse growth required to
compensate for the shrinkage caused by the exceptional block.

If a later segment has leading reverse multiplier \(G>1\), then it
reduces the outstanding debt by a factor of \(G\):
\[
B\longmapsto \frac{B}{G}.
\]
The debt is cleared once
\[
B<1.
\]

After a finite \(\Omega_2\) self-loop run, the orbit exits to either
\((o,0)\) or \((o,1)\).  The corresponding one-step growing return
multipliers are
\[
(o,0):\qquad G_{\Omega_0}=\frac{32}{3},
\]
and
\[
(o,1):\qquad G_{\Omega_1}=\frac{8}{3}.
\]
Since
\[
\frac{8}{3}<\frac{32}{3},
\]
the \((o,1)\) exit gives the conservative worst-case compensation rate.

Suppose therefore that we compensate a \(k\)-step exceptional run using
\(r\) worst-case \((o,1)\)-strength returns.  The remaining debt is
\[
B_{k,r}
=
\frac{(3/2)^k}{(8/3)^r}.
\]
The debt is cleared when
\[
B_{k,r}<1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\frac{(3/2)^k}{(8/3)^r}<1,
\]
or
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
r\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
>
k\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right),
\]
so it is enough that
\[
\boxed{
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}.
}
\]
Numerically,
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414.
\]
Thus, at the level of leading multipliers, approximately \(41.4\%\) as
many \((o,1)\)-strength returns as exceptional \((o,2)\) loops are enough
to compensate the exceptional shrinkage.

For a cleaner sufficient bound, we may use
\[
r=\left\lceil \frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
\]
Since
\[
\frac{1}{2}>
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)},
\]
this choice is safely sufficient.  Therefore
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k,
\]
and hence
\[
\frac{(3/2)^k}{(8/3)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}}<1.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Worst-case compensation budget]
\label{lem:worst-case-compensation-budget}
Let a positive orbit contain a finite exceptional block
\[
\Omega_2^k.
\]
At the leading-multiplier level, this block creates growth debt
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Then
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil
\]
worst-case \((o,1)\)-strength growing returns are sufficient to clear
this debt.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Each exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop has reverse multiplier
\[
\frac{2}{3},
\]
so \(k\) such loops have reverse multiplier
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
Equivalently, they create forward growth debt
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]

The conservative post-exception return multiplier is the \((o,1)\) value
\[
G_{\Omega_1}=\frac{8}{3}.
\]
After \(r\) such returns, the remaining debt is
\[
B_{k,r}
=
\frac{(3/2)^k}{(8/3)^r}.
\]
It is enough to show that this is \(<1\) for
\[
r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
\]
Since
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil\ge \frac{k}{2},
\]
we have
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
\ge
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{k/2}.
\]
Thus it is enough to check
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{k/2}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking the \(k\)-th root, this is equivalent to
\[
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}>\frac{3}{2}.
\]
Squaring both sides gives
\[
\frac{8}{3}>\frac{9}{4}.
\]
Multiplying by \(12\), this becomes
\[
32>27,
\]
which is true.  Therefore
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k,
\]
and so
\[
B_{k,\lceil k/2\rceil}<1.
\]
Hence \(\lceil k/2\rceil\) worst-case \((o,1)\)-strength returns clear
the growth debt created by \(k\) exceptional \(\Omega_2\) loops.
\end{proof}

For example,
\[
k=2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil=1,
\]
and indeed
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^2
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{32}{27}>1.
\]
For
\[
k=3,
\]
one \((o,1)\)-strength return is not enough:
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^3
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
=
\frac{64}{81}<1.
\]
But
\[
\left\lceil\frac{3}{2}\right\rceil=2,
\]
and two such returns give
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^3
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^2
=
\frac{512}{243}>1.
\]
Similarly,
\[
k=5
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil=3,
\]
and
\[
k=10
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil=5.
\]

This debt-budget lemma is still a leading-multiplier result.  The exact
word-level inequality remains
\[
C_W^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_W}-3^{|W|}\right)m_n.
\]
However, the relevant \((o,1)\) and \((o,0)\) return segments have
nonpositive carry terms, so their carry contributions assist the
reverse-growth inequality rather than obstructing it.

\subsubsection{Debt compensation after an exceptional run}
\label{subsubsec:debt-compensation-lemma}

We now formalize the compensation principle suggested by the exceptional
\((o,2)\) analysis.  A run of \(k\) consecutive exceptional
\(\Omega_2\) self-loops has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_2^k}
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
Thus, in reverse, the exceptional block shrinks by the factor
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
Equivalently, it creates a forward-growth debt of
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]

Once the exceptional run exits, it enters either \((o,0)\) or \((o,1)\).
The corresponding same-symbol growing returns have leading reverse
multipliers
\[
G_{\Omega_0}=\frac{32}{3},
\qquad
G_{\Omega_1}=\frac{8}{3}.
\]
Since
\[
\frac{8}{3}<\frac{32}{3},
\]
the \((o,1)\) return gives the conservative worst-case compensation
rate.  Therefore, to prove a worst-case compensation estimate, it is
enough to show that sufficiently many \((o,1)\)-strength returns
overcome the debt created by the exceptional \(\Omega_2\)-run.

\begin{lemma}[Debt compensation]
\label{lem:debt-compensation}
Let \(k\ge 1\).  A block of \(k\) exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loops
creates growth debt
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Then
\[
r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil
\]
worst-case \((o,1)\)-strength growing returns are sufficient to clear
this debt.  Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>1.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The exceptional block \(\Omega_2^k\) has leading reverse multiplier
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
Each \((o,1)\)-strength compensating return has leading reverse
multiplier
\[
\frac{8}{3}.
\]
Thus \(r\) such compensating returns produce combined multiplier
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r.
\]
We want this to be greater than \(1\).

Set
\[
r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
\]
Since
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil\ge \frac{k}{2},
\]
we have
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
\ge
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{k/2}.
\]
Therefore it is enough to prove
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{k/2}
>1.
\]
Rewrite the left-hand side as
\[
\left[
\frac{2}{3}
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{1/2}
\right]^k.
\]
Thus it is enough to show
\[
\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}>\frac{3}{2}.
\]
Squaring both sides gives
\[
\frac{8}{3}>\frac{9}{4}.
\]
Multiplying by \(12\), this becomes
\[
32>27,
\]
which is true.  Hence
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>1.
\]
Therefore \(\lceil k/2\rceil\) worst-case \((o,1)\)-strength returns
clear the leading growth debt created by \(k\) exceptional
\(\Omega_2\) self-loops.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, one may express the exact threshold by solving
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}.
\]
Since
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414,
\]
approximately \(41.4\%\) as many \((o,1)\)-strength returns as
exceptional \((o,2)\) loops are enough at the leading-multiplier level.
The simpler bound
\[
r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil
\]
is slightly stronger but easier to use.

This lemma is a leading-multiplier statement.  The exact word-level
criterion remains
\[
C_W^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_W}-3^{|W|}\right)m_n.
\]
However, the relevant \((o,1)\) and \((o,0)\) return segments have
nonpositive carry terms, so their accumulated carry contributions assist
rather than obstruct reverse growth.


\subsubsection{Residue-level transitions from the exit symbols}
\label{subsubsec:exit-symbol-transition-table}

After a finite exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop run, the orbit exits
from \((o,2)\) to either \((o,0)\) or \((o,1)\).  We therefore examine
the exact residue-level behavior of these two exit symbols.

Throughout this subsection we work at \(p=0\).  Write
\[
m=3M+r,
\qquad
r\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
For a row with base exponent \(\alpha\) and base constant \(k^{(0)}\),
the output router is determined by
\[
j'\equiv 2^\alpha r+k^{(0)}\pmod 3.
\]
The output index has the form
\[
m'=2^\alpha M+t_r,
\]
where \(t_r\) is the carry determined by the corresponding row.

For the exit symbol \((o,0)\), the relevant \(p=0\) rows are
\[
\omega_0:(o,0)\to e,
\qquad
\Omega_0:(o,0)\to o.
\]
For the exit symbol \((o,1)\), the relevant \(p=0\) rows are
\[
\omega_1:(o,1)\to e,
\qquad
\Omega_1:(o,1)\to o.
\]

The resulting residue-level transitions are shown in
Table~\ref{tab:exit-symbol-residue-transitions}.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\footnotesize
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.12}
\caption{Residue-level \(p=0\) transitions from the exit symbols \((o,0)\) and \((o,1)\), where \(r=m\bmod 3\).}
\label{tab:exit-symbol-residue-transitions}

\begin{tabular}{@{}c c c c@{}}
\toprule
Input & Row & \(r\) & Output \\
\midrule
\((o,0)\) & \(\omega_0\) & \(0\) & \((e,2)\) \\
\((o,0)\) & \(\omega_0\) & \(1\) & \((e,1)\) \\
\((o,0)\) & \(\omega_0\) & \(2\) & \((e,0)\) \\
\midrule
\((o,0)\) & \(\Omega_0\) & \(0\) & \((o,2)\) \\
\((o,0)\) & \(\Omega_0\) & \(1\) & \((o,1)\) \\
\((o,0)\) & \(\Omega_0\) & \(2\) & \((o,0)\) \\
\midrule
\((o,1)\) & \(\omega_1\) & \(0\) & \((e,1)\) \\
\((o,1)\) & \(\omega_1\) & \(1\) & \((e,0)\) \\
\((o,1)\) & \(\omega_1\) & \(2\) & \((e,2)\) \\
\midrule
\((o,1)\) & \(\Omega_1\) & \(0\) & \((o,1)\) \\
\((o,1)\) & \(\Omega_1\) & \(1\) & \((o,0)\) \\
\((o,1)\) & \(\Omega_1\) & \(2\) & \((o,2)\) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For \((o,0)\), the same-symbol growing return is
\[
(o,0)\xrightarrow{\Omega_0}(o,0).
\]
From the table, this occurs when
\[
r=2,
\qquad
m=3M+2.
\]
For \(\Omega_0\), the base exponent is
\[
\alpha=5,
\]
and the output index in the \(r=2\) case is
\[
m'=32M+24.
\]
Therefore
\[
m'-m
=
(32M+24)-(3M+2)
=
29M+22>0.
\]
Thus this same-symbol return is strongly reverse-growing.

For \((o,1)\), the same-symbol growing return is
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1).
\]
From the table, this occurs when
\[
r=0,
\qquad
m=3M.
\]
For \(\Omega_1\), the base exponent is
\[
\alpha=3,
\]
and the output index in the \(r=0\) case is
\[
m'=8M+1.
\]
Therefore
\[
m'-m
=
(8M+1)-3M
=
5M+1>0.
\]
Thus this same-symbol return is also reverse-growing.

The table has two important consequences.  First, after a finite
exceptional \(\Omega_2\) run exits to \((o,0)\) or \((o,1)\), both exit
symbols have available same-symbol reverse-growing returns.  Second,
these same-symbol returns are residue-specific:
\[
(o,0)\xrightarrow{\Omega_0}(o,0)
\quad\text{requires}\quad
m\equiv 2\pmod 3,
\]
whereas
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\quad\text{requires}\quad
m\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
Thus the next coverage problem is residue-level: after exiting an
exceptional \((o,2)\) run, one must determine which residues occur and
how quickly they route into one of the available growing returns.


\subsubsection{Separate compensation bounds for the two exit symbols}
\label{subsubsec:separate-exit-compensation-bounds}

After a finite exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loop run, the orbit exits
from \((o,2)\) to either \((o,0)\) or \((o,1)\).  These two exits have
different compensating strengths.  The \((o,0)\) exit is stronger, while
the \((o,1)\) exit is the conservative worst case.

A block of \(k\) exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loops creates growth debt
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
A compensating return segment with reverse multiplier \(G>1\) reduces
this debt by a factor of \(G\).  Thus \(r\) such returns clear the debt
provided
\[
G^r>\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]

\paragraph{The \((o,0)\) exit.}

The same-symbol return
\[
(o,0)\xrightarrow{\Omega_0}(o,0)
\]
has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_0}
=
\frac{2^5}{3}
=
\frac{32}{3}.
\]
Thus \(r\) \((o,0)\)-strength returns compensate \(k\) exceptional
\(\Omega_2\) self-loops if
\[
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^r
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives the exact threshold
\[
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(32/3)}.
\]
Numerically,
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(32/3)}
\approx 0.171.
\]
So approximately \(17.1\%\) as many \((o,0)\)-strength returns as
exceptional \((o,2)\) loops are enough at the leading-multiplier level.

For a simple integer bound, it is enough to take
\[
r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{5}\right\rceil.
\]

\begin{lemma}[\((o,0)\)-exit compensation]
\label{lem:o0-exit-compensation}
Let \(k\ge 1\).  A block of \(k\) exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loops
creates growth debt
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Then
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{5}\right\rceil
\]
\((o,0)\)-strength returns are sufficient to clear this debt.  Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/5\rceil}
>1.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It is enough to show that
\[
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/5\rceil}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Since
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{5}\right\rceil\ge \frac{k}{5},
\]
we have
\[
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/5\rceil}
\ge
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{k/5}.
\]
Thus it is enough to prove
\[
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{k/5}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking the \(k\)-th root, this is equivalent to
\[
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{1/5}
>
\frac{3}{2}.
\]
Raising both sides to the fifth power, this becomes
\[
\frac{32}{3}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^5.
\]
Since
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^5=\frac{243}{32},
\]
the desired inequality is
\[
\frac{32}{3}>\frac{243}{32}.
\]
Multiplying by \(96\), this becomes
\[
1024>729,
\]
which is true.  Therefore
\[
\left(\frac{32}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/5\rceil}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k,
\]
so the debt is cleared.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The \((o,1)\) exit.}

The same-symbol return
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\]
has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_1}
=
\frac{2^3}{3}
=
\frac{8}{3}.
\]
This is weaker than the \((o,0)\) return, so it gives the conservative
worst-case exit compensation.

Thus \(r\) \((o,1)\)-strength returns compensate \(k\) exceptional
\(\Omega_2\) self-loops if
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}.
\]
Numerically,
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414.
\]
So approximately \(41.4\%\) as many \((o,1)\)-strength returns as
exceptional \((o,2)\) loops are enough at the leading-multiplier level.

For a clean integer bound, it is enough to take
\[
r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
\]

\begin{lemma}[\((o,1)\)-exit compensation]
\label{lem:o1-exit-compensation}
Let \(k\ge 1\).  A block of \(k\) exceptional \(\Omega_2\) self-loops
creates growth debt
\[
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Then
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil
\]
\((o,1)\)-strength returns are sufficient to clear this debt.  Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>1.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
It is enough to show that
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Since
\[
\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil\ge \frac{k}{2},
\]
we have
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
\ge
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{k/2}.
\]
Thus it is enough to prove
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{k/2}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking the \(k\)-th root, this is equivalent to
\[
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}
>
\frac{3}{2}.
\]
Squaring both sides gives
\[
\frac{8}{3}>\frac{9}{4}.
\]
Multiplying by \(12\), this becomes
\[
32>27,
\]
which is true.  Therefore
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{\lceil k/2\rceil}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k,
\]
so the debt is cleared.
\end{proof}

The two lemmas show that the \((o,0)\) exit is much easier to compensate
than the \((o,1)\) exit:
\[
(o,0):\qquad r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{5}\right\rceil,
\]
whereas
\[
(o,1):\qquad r=\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
\]
Thus the \((o,1)\) exit remains the conservative worst case for the
post-exception compensation argument.

As before, these are leading-multiplier estimates.  The exact
word-level criterion is still
\[
C_W^{(0)}
<
\left(2^{A_W}-3^{|W|}\right)m_n.
\]
However, the relevant \((o,0)\) and \((o,1)\) same-symbol returns have
nonpositive carry contributions, so their carry terms assist reverse
growth rather than obstruct it.

\subsubsection{Exact structure of \((o,1)\)-runs}
\label{subsubsec:exact-o1-runs}

We now analyze the \((o,1)\) loop in the same way that we analyzed the
exceptional \((o,2)\) loop.  The important difference is that the
\((o,1)\) loop is not an obstruction: it is a reverse-growing loop.  It
therefore acts as a compensating mechanism for the debt created by
exceptional \((o,2)\) runs.

The same-symbol \((o,1)\) return is
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1).
\]
At \(p=0\), the row \(\Omega_1\) has
\[
\alpha=3,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=4.
\]
Thus
\[
F(0,m)=8m+4.
\]
The output router is obtained from
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+j',
\qquad
j'\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
Since
\[
j'\equiv 8m+4\pmod 3,
\]
and
\[
8\equiv 2\pmod 3,
\qquad
4\equiv 1\pmod 3,
\]
we have
\[
j'\equiv 2m+1\pmod 3.
\]
The \((o,1)\) self-return occurs exactly when
\[
j'=1.
\]
Therefore
\[
2m+1\equiv 1\pmod 3,
\]
which is equivalent to
\[
m\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
m\equiv 0\pmod 3.
}
\]

If
\[
m=3M,
\]
then
\[
F(0,m)=8(3M)+4=24M+4.
\]
Since the output router is \(j'=1\), we write
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+1.
\]
Hence
\[
3m'+1=24M+4,
\]
so
\[
m'=8M+1.
\]
In terms of \(m\), this is
\[
\boxed{
m'=\frac{8m+3}{3}.
}
\]

This affine recurrence has a useful shifted form.  Starting from
\[
m'=\frac{8m+3}{3},
\]
we compute
\[
5m'+3
=
5\left(\frac{8m+3}{3}\right)+3
=
\frac{40m+15+9}{3}
=
\frac{40m+24}{3}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{
5m'+3=\frac{8}{3}(5m+3).
}
\]

\begin{lemma}[Exact \((o,1)\)-run formula]
\label{lem:exact-o1-run-formula}
Suppose an orbit remains in the \((o,1)\) same-symbol loop
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\]
for \(r\) consecutive steps.  Then
\[
\boxed{
5m_r+3
=
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r(5m_0+3).
}
\]
In particular, \(r\) consecutive \((o,1)\) loops are possible only if
\[
3^r\mid 5m_0+3.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For one \((o,1)\) self-return, we have shown that
\[
5m_{i+1}+3
=
\frac{8}{3}(5m_i+3).
\]
Iterating this identity \(r\) times gives
\[
5m_r+3
=
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r(5m_0+3).
\]
For \(m_r\) to be an integer after \(r\) such steps, the denominator
\(3^r\) must divide \(5m_0+3\).  Hence
\[
3^r\mid 5m_0+3.
\]
\end{proof}

The exact length of an \((o,1)\)-run is therefore measured by the
\(3\)-adic valuation of \(5m+3\).

\begin{lemma}[Exact length of an \((o,1)\)-run]
\label{lem:exact-length-o1-run}
An \((o,1)\) same-symbol run has exact length \(r\) if and only if
\[
v_3(5m_0+3)=r.
\]
Equivalently, there is an integer \(q\) with
\[
3\nmid q
\]
such that
\[
\boxed{
5m_0+3=3^r q.
}
\]
After the \(r\)-step \((o,1)\) run, one has
\[
\boxed{
5m_r+3=8^r q.
}
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
m_r=\frac{8^r q-3}{5}.
}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:exact-o1-run-formula}, \(r\) consecutive
\((o,1)\)-loops require
\[
3^r\mid 5m_0+3.
\]
The run has exact length \(r\) precisely when the divisibility holds for
\(3^r\), but fails for \(3^{r+1}\).  This is exactly
\[
v_3(5m_0+3)=r.
\]
Thus we may write
\[
5m_0+3=3^r q,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
\]
Substituting this into the \(r\)-step formula gives
\[
5m_r+3
=
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r3^r q
=
8^r q.
\]
Solving for \(m_r\) gives
\[
m_r=\frac{8^r q-3}{5}.
\]
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Exit from a finite \((o,1)\)-run}
\label{subsubsec:exit-from-o1-run}

After an exact \((o,1)\)-run terminates, the next \(\Omega_1\)-transition
cannot remain in \((o,1)\).  Therefore the run must exit to one of the
other two \(o\)-symbols.

For \(\Omega_1\), the residue-level routing is
\[
(o,1)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}
\begin{cases}
(o,1), & m\equiv 0\pmod 3,\\
(o,0), & m\equiv 1\pmod 3,\\
(o,2), & m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\end{cases}
\]

\begin{lemma}[Exit from a finite \((o,1)\)-run]
\label{lem:exit-from-finite-o1-run}
Let an orbit remain in the \((o,1)\) same-symbol loop for exactly
\(r\ge 1\) steps.  Then after the \(r\)-th loop, the next
\(\Omega_1\)-transition exits to either
\[
(o,0)
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
(o,2).
\]
It cannot remain in \((o,1)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:exact-length-o1-run}, an exact \(r\)-step
\((o,1)\)-run satisfies
\[
5m_0+3=3^r q,
\qquad
3\nmid q,
\]
and after \(r\) steps,
\[
5m_r+3=8^r q.
\]
If the loop could continue, then we would have
\[
m_r\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
This would imply
\[
5m_r+3\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
But
\[
5m_r+3=8^r q.
\]
Since
\[
8\equiv 2\pmod 3
\]
and
\[
3\nmid q,
\]
we have
\[
8^r q\not\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
This is a contradiction.  Hence
\[
m_r\not\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
Therefore
\[
m_r\equiv 1\pmod 3
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
m_r\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Under the \(\Omega_1\) routing,
\[
m_r\equiv 1\pmod 3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(o,1)\to(o,0),
\]
and
\[
m_r\equiv 2\pmod 3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(o,1)\to(o,2).
\]
Thus the exact finite \((o,1)\)-run exits to either \((o,0)\) or
\((o,2)\), and cannot continue as another \((o,1)\) loop.
\end{proof}

The exit to \((o,0)\) is favorable, since \((o,0)\) has a strong
same-symbol growing return.  The exit to \((o,2)\) may begin a new
exceptional block, so this is the case that must be tracked by the debt
ledger.


\subsubsection{Debt update for alternating \((o,2)\) and \((o,1)\) blocks}
\label{subsubsec:alternating-debt-update}

The preceding lemmas show that long runs of \((o,2)\) and \((o,1)\)
have exact affine forms and exact finite exit rules.  The natural
remaining object is therefore an alternating sequence of blocks:
\[
(o,2)^{k_1}
\to
(o,1)^{r_1}
\to
(o,2)^{k_2}
\to
(o,1)^{r_2}
\to
\cdots .
\]
Here the \((o,2)\)-blocks create debt, while the \((o,1)\)-blocks repay
debt.

A block of \(k_i\) exceptional \((o,2)\) self-loops has reverse
multiplier
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{k_i}.
\]
Since this multiplier is less than \(1\), it creates anti-growth.  If
debt is measured as the reciprocal of reverse growth, then this block
increases debt by
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{-k_i}
=
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{k_i}.
\]

A block of \(r_i\) \((o,1)\) self-returns has reverse multiplier
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{r_i}.
\]
Since this multiplier is greater than \(1\), it supplies compensating
growth.  Thus it repays debt by a growth factor
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{r_i}.
\]
Equivalently, if debt itself is tracked multiplicatively, then the same
\((o,1)^{r_i}\)-block multiplies the remaining debt by the reciprocal
factor
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{-r_i}
=
\left(\frac{3}{8}\right)^{r_i}.
\]

Define the logarithmic debt after \(N\) alternating blocks by
\[
\boxed{
\mathcal D_N
=
\sum_{i=1}^{N} k_i\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_i\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
}
\]
Equivalently, the multiplicative debt is
\[
\boxed{
B_N
=
\prod_{i=1}^{N}
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{k_i}
\left(\frac{3}{8}\right)^{r_i}.
}
\]
These conventions are consistent because
\[
B_N=e^{\mathcal D_N}.
\]

The debt is cleared once
\[
B_N<1,
\]
or equivalently,
\[
\mathcal D_N<0.
\]

Thus an alternating exceptional path can remain uncompensated only if
\[
\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_i\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
\le
\sum_{i=1}^{N} k_i\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
\]
for every initial block length \(N\).  Equivalently,
\[
\sum_{i=1}^{N} r_i
\le
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\sum_{i=1}^{N} k_i.
\]
Since
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414,
\]
the debt remains uncleared only if the total number of \((o,1)\)-growth
steps stays below approximately \(41.4\%\) of the total number of
exceptional \((o,2)\)-steps.

This converts the remaining obstruction into a precise accounting
problem: one must rule out an infinite admissible alternation in which
the \((o,1)\)-growth blocks are persistently too short to compensate the
\((o,2)\)-debt blocks.  The exit lemmas above show that both kinds of
blocks are finite in the positive integers; the remaining issue is the
relative size of the successive block lengths.



\subsubsection{Short repayment and the block-to-block transition}
\label{subsubsec:short-repayment-block-transition}

We now combine the \((o,2)\)-run and \((o,1)\)-run analyses into a
single block-to-block calculation.  The purpose is to understand what
happens when an exceptional \((o,2)\)-run exits to \((o,1)\), but the
resulting \((o,1)\)-run is too short to repay the debt created by the
preceding exceptional block.

Suppose an exact \((o,2)\)-run has length \(k\).  Then the initial
\((o,2)\) index has the form
\[
m_0+1=3^k q,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m_0=3^k q-1.
\]
After \(k\) consecutive \(\Omega_2\) self-loops, we have
\[
m_k+1=2^k q,
\]
so
\[
m_k=2^k q-1.
\]

The run exits to \((o,1)\) precisely when
\[
m_k\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]
Using the formula for \(m_k\), this is equivalent to
\[
2^k q-1\equiv 0\pmod 3,
\]
or
\[
2^k q\equiv 1\pmod 3.
\]

Now suppose that after this exit, the orbit remains in the
\((o,1)\)-loop for \(r\) consecutive steps.  The length of this
\((o,1)\)-run is measured by
\[
r=v_3(5m_k+3).
\]
Substituting
\[
m_k=2^k q-1,
\]
we get
\[
5m_k+3
=
5(2^k q-1)+3
=
5\cdot 2^k q-2.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{
r=v_3(5\cdot 2^k q-2).
}
\]

Thus an \((o,1)\)-run of length at least \(r\) occurs precisely when
\[
3^r\mid 5\cdot 2^k q-2.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
5\cdot 2^k q\equiv 2\pmod {3^r}.
\]
Since \(5\cdot 2^k\) is invertible modulo \(3^r\), this determines \(q\)
modulo \(3^r\):
\[
\boxed{
q\equiv
2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}
\pmod {3^r}.
}
\]
Therefore the original \((o,2)\) starting index must satisfy
\[
\boxed{
m_0=3^kq-1,
\qquad
q\equiv
2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}
\pmod {3^r}.
}
\]
Equivalently, \(m_0\) lies in a single residue class modulo
\[
3^{k+r}.
\]

This gives an important case distinction.  Let
\[
R_k=\left\lceil \frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
\]
By the debt-compensation lemma, an \((o,1)\)-run of length \(R_k\) is
sufficient, at the leading-multiplier level, to compensate the debt
created by the preceding \((o,2)^k\)-run.  Therefore, if
\[
r\ge R_k,
\]
then the block
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r
\]
has repaid the exceptional debt.

If instead
\[
r<R_k,
\]
then the immediate \((o,1)\)-run is too short to repay the preceding
\((o,2)^k\)-debt.  In that case the congruence above holds only up to
the shorter power \(3^r\), but not up to the repayment threshold
\(3^{R_k}\):
\[
q\equiv
2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}
\pmod {3^r},
\]
while
\[
q\not\equiv
2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}
\pmod {3^{R_k}}.
\]
Thus the short-repayment case is arithmetically visible: it corresponds
to an initial \((o,2)\) index lying in the residue class required for an
\((o,1)^r\)-run, but not in the narrower class required for an
\((o,1)^{R_k}\)-run.

If the short \((o,1)\)-run exits to \((o,0)\), then the orbit enters the
stronger exit channel, whose compensating multiplier is \(32/3\).  The
more delicate case is when the short \((o,1)\)-run exits back to
\((o,2)\), beginning another possible exceptional block.  We now compute
the corresponding next \((o,2)\) starting index.

Set
\[
s=\frac{5\cdot 2^kq-2}{3^r},
\qquad
3\nmid s.
\]
Then
\[
5m_k+3=3^r s.
\]
After the \(r\)-step \((o,1)\)-run, the exact \((o,1)\)-run formula gives
\[
5m_r+3=8^r s.
\]
Hence
\[
m_r=\frac{8^r s-3}{5}.
\]

If the run exits from \((o,1)\) to \((o,2)\), then the next \((o,2)\)
input index is obtained from the \(\Omega_1\)-transition in the
\(m\equiv 2\pmod 3\) case.  This gives
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8m_r+2}{3}.
\]
Substituting the formula for \(m_r\), we obtain
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8\left(\frac{8^r s-3}{5}\right)+2}{3}.
\]
Therefore
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s-24+10}{15},
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s-14}{15}.
}
\]
Since
\[
s=\frac{5\cdot 2^kq-2}{3^r},
\]
this gives the explicit block-to-block transition
\[
\boxed{
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{
8^{r+1}
\left(\frac{5\cdot 2^kq-2}{3^r}\right)
-14
}{15}.
}
\]

The length of the next exceptional \((o,2)\)-run is
\[
k_{\mathrm{next}}
=
v_3(m_{\mathrm{next}}+1).
\]
Using the formula above,
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}+1
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s-14}{15}+1
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s+1}{15}.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
k_{\mathrm{next}}
=
v_3(8^{r+1}s+1)-1.
}
\]

We have therefore obtained a precise recurrence for the dangerous
short-repayment case:
\[
q
\longmapsto
s=\frac{5\cdot 2^kq-2}{3^r},
\]
\[
s
\longmapsto
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s-14}{15},
\]
and
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}
\longmapsto
k_{\mathrm{next}}
=
v_3(m_{\mathrm{next}}+1).
\]

This is the arithmetic engine for the remaining obstruction.  If the
\((o,1)\)-run is long enough, the preceding \((o,2)\)-debt is repaid.  If
it is too short and exits to \((o,0)\), the orbit enters the stronger
compensating channel.  The only delicate continuation is the one in
which a too-short \((o,1)\)-run exits back to \((o,2)\).  In that case,
the next exceptional length is not arbitrary; it is determined by the
explicit valuation formula
\[
k_{\mathrm{next}}
=
v_3(8^{r+1}s+1)-1.
\]
Thus repeated short repayment would require an infinite iteration of
these increasingly restrictive arithmetic conditions.

\subsection{Exceptional \((o,2)\)-runs, \((o,1)\)-compensation, and cumulative debt}
\label{subsec:o2-o1-debt-analysis}

We now isolate the remaining obstruction more sharply.  The difficult
case is the exceptional \((o,2)\) self-loop at \(p=0\).  The analysis
below shows that this self-loop cannot persist indefinitely in the
positive integers.  It also introduces a debt framework for measuring
how much subsequent \((o,1)\)-growth is needed to compensate a finite
exceptional \((o,2)\)-run.

\subsubsection{The exceptional \((o,2)\) self-loop}

The symbol \((o,2)\) corresponds to odd integers of the form
\[
x=18m+17.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
x\equiv 17\pmod {18}.
\]

For the exceptional row
\[
\Omega_2:(o,2)\to(o,2)
\]
at \(p=0\), we have
\[
\alpha=1,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=1.
\]
Thus
\[
F(0,m)=2m+1.
\]
The output router is determined by
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+j',
\qquad
j'\in\{0,1,2\}.
\]
Since
\[
j'\equiv 2m+1\pmod 3,
\]
the self-loop condition \(j'=2\) is
\[
2m+1\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
Hence
\[
m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]

If \(m=3M+2\), then
\[
F(0,m)=2(3M+2)+1=6M+5.
\]
Since the self-loop has \(j'=2\), we write
\[
6M+5=3m'+2,
\]
so
\[
m'=2M+1.
\]
In terms of \(m\), this is
\[
m'=\frac{2m-1}{3}.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\boxed{
m'+1=\frac{2}{3}(m+1).
}
\]

Thus after \(k\) consecutive \((o,2)\) self-loops,
\[
\boxed{
m_k+1=\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k(m_0+1).
}
\]

For these \(k\) loops to be possible in the ordinary integers, we need
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
Therefore a \(k\)-step exceptional run requires
\[
\boxed{
m_0\equiv -1\pmod {3^k}.
}
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m_0+1=3^kq
\]
for some integer \(q\).  For an exact run of length \(k\), we require
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1
\quad\text{but}\quad
3^{k+1}\nmid m_0+1.
\]
Thus
\[
\boxed{
m_0=3^kq-1,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
}
\]
After the \(k\)-step run,
\[
m_k+1=2^kq,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
m_k=2^kq-1.
}
\]

In terms of the original odd integer
\[
x_0=18m_0+17,
\]
we have
\[
x_0+1=18(m_0+1).
\]
Therefore \(k\) consecutive exceptional \((o,2)\)-loops require
\[
\boxed{
x_0\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}.
}
\]

For the first few values:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Number of consecutive }\Omega_2\text{ loops} &
\text{Required congruence for }x_0\\
\hline
1 & x_0\equiv 53\pmod {54}\\
2 & x_0\equiv 161\pmod {162}\\
3 & x_0\equiv 485\pmod {486}\\
k & x_0\equiv -1\pmod {18\cdot 3^k}.
\end{array}
\]

Thus each additional exceptional self-loop narrows the possible input
class by another factor of \(3\).

\subsubsection{No infinite positive \((o,2)\) self-loop}

The preceding formula immediately rules out an infinite positive
\((o,2)\)-self-loop.

\begin{lemma}[No infinite positive \((o,2)\) self-loop]
\label{lem:pure-o2-finite}
No positive integer state can remain forever in the exceptional
\[
(o,2)\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}(o,2)
\]
self-loop.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If a positive integer remained in the \((o,2)\) self-loop forever, then
for every \(k\ge 1\) we would have
\[
3^k\mid m_0+1.
\]
The only ordinary integer divisible by every power of \(3\) is \(0\).
Thus
\[
m_0+1=0,
\]
so
\[
m_0=-1.
\]
But in the \((o,2)\) class,
\[
x_0=18m_0+17,
\]
and therefore
\[
x_0=18(-1)+17=-1.
\]
This is outside the positive Collatz domain.  Hence no positive integer
can remain indefinitely in the \((o,2)\) self-loop.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, a long \((o,2)\)-run forces
\[
m_0\equiv -1\pmod {3^k}.
\]
Thus \(m_0\) becomes closer and closer to \(-1\) in the \(3\)-adic sense,
although in the ordinary real sense the value \(m_0=3^kq-1\) becomes
large when \(q>0\).

\subsubsection{Exit from a finite \((o,2)\)-run}

After an exact \((o,2)^k\)-run, we have
\[
m_k=2^kq-1,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
\]
The self-loop would continue only if
\[
m_k\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\]
But an exact \(k\)-step run terminates, so this congruence fails.  Hence
\[
m_k\equiv 0
\quad\text{or}\quad
m_k\equiv 1
\pmod 3.
\]

For the \(\Omega_2\) row, the residue-level routing is
\[
(o,2)
\xrightarrow{\Omega_2}
\begin{cases}
(o,1), & m\equiv 0\pmod 3,\\
(o,0), & m\equiv 1\pmod 3,\\
(o,2), & m\equiv 2\pmod 3.
\end{cases}
\]
Therefore every finite exceptional \((o,2)\)-run exits to one of two
symbols:
\[
\boxed{
(o,1)\quad\text{or}\quad(o,0).
}
\]

The exit to \((o,0)\) is favorable, since the same-symbol return
\[
(o,0)\xrightarrow{\Omega_0}(o,0)
\]
has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_0}=\frac{2^5}{3}=\frac{32}{3}.
\]
The exit to \((o,1)\) is the conservative, weaker exit, since
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1)
\]
has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G_{\Omega_1}=\frac{2^3}{3}=\frac{8}{3}.
\]

\subsubsection{Debt created by an \((o,2)^k\)-run}

A single \((o,2)\) loop has reverse multiplier
\[
\frac{2}{3}.
\]
Thus \(k\) consecutive \((o,2)\)-loops have reverse multiplier
\[
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k.
\]
This is reverse shrinkage.  It is useful to measure the corresponding
forward-growth debt by the reciprocal factor
\[
\boxed{
B_k=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
}
\]

In logarithmic form, define
\[
D=\log B.
\]
Then one \((o,2)\) loop changes the debt by
\[
\boxed{
\Delta D_{o,2}=\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right).
}
\]
Thus the debt increases at a constant rate per \((o,2)\) loop.

\subsubsection{The \((o,1)\)-run and its repayment rate}

The same-symbol \((o,1)\) return is
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\Omega_1}(o,1).
\]
At \(p=0\), this row has
\[
\alpha=3,
\qquad
k^{(0)}=4.
\]
Thus
\[
F(0,m)=8m+4.
\]
Since
\[
F(0,m)=3m'+j',
\]
we have
\[
j'\equiv 8m+4\pmod 3.
\]
Using
\[
8\equiv 2\pmod 3,
\qquad
4\equiv 1\pmod 3,
\]
we obtain
\[
j'\equiv 2m+1\pmod 3.
\]
The same-symbol return requires \(j'=1\), so
\[
2m+1\equiv 1\pmod 3,
\]
which is equivalent to
\[
m\equiv 0\pmod 3.
\]

If \(m=3M\), then
\[
F(0,m)=8(3M)+4=24M+4.
\]
Since \(j'=1\),
\[
24M+4=3m'+1,
\]
so
\[
m'=8M+1.
\]
In terms of \(m\),
\[
m'=\frac{8m+3}{3}.
\]

A useful shifted form is
\[
\boxed{
5m'+3=\frac{8}{3}(5m+3).
}
\]
Therefore after \(r\) consecutive \((o,1)\)-loops,
\[
\boxed{
5m_r+3=
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r(5m_0+3).
}
\]

Thus one \((o,1)\) loop repays debt by the reverse-growth factor
\[
\frac{8}{3}.
\]
Equivalently, multiplicative debt changes by
\[
B\mapsto \frac{3}{8}B.
\]
In logarithmic debt,
\[
\boxed{
\Delta D_{o,1}=-\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
}
\]

Numerically,
\[
\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)\approx 0.4055,
\qquad
\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)\approx 0.9808.
\]
Thus one \((o,1)\) loop repays about
\[
\frac{\log(8/3)}{\log(3/2)}
\approx 2.416
\]
\((o,2)\)-loops worth of debt.

\subsubsection{One \((o,2)^k(o,1)^r\) block}

Now consider one alternating block
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r.
\]
The \((o,2)^k\)-block creates debt
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k,
\]
while the following \((o,1)^r\)-block repays by
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r.
\]
The combined leading reverse multiplier is
\[
\boxed{
G(k,r)=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r.
}
\]
The block repays its debt exactly when
\[
G(k,r)>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
r\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)>
k\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right),
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}.
}
\]
Since
\[
\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414,
\]
we have the repayment criterion
\[
\boxed{
r>0.414k.
}
\]
A cleaner sufficient bound is
\[
\boxed{
r\ge \left\lceil \frac{k}{2}\right\rceil.
}
\]

However, \(r\) is not arbitrary.  If the exact \((o,2)^k\)-run begins at
\[
m_0=3^kq-1,
\qquad
3\nmid q,
\]
then after the \((o,2)^k\)-run,
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]
If the run exits to \((o,1)\), then
\[
m_k\equiv 0\pmod 3,
\]
or
\[
2^kq\equiv 1\pmod 3.
\]

The length of the following \((o,1)\)-run is
\[
r=v_3(5m_k+3).
\]
Substituting \(m_k=2^kq-1\), we obtain
\[
5m_k+3
=
5(2^kq-1)+3
=
5\cdot 2^kq-2.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{
r=v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2).
}
\]

Therefore the one-block repayment criterion becomes
\[
\boxed{
v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2)>0.414k.
}
\]
Using the cleaner sufficient criterion,
\[
\boxed{
v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2)\ge \left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil
}
\]
guarantees repayment.

The complementary case
\[
v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2)<\left\lceil\frac{k}{2}\right\rceil
\]
does not mean repayment is impossible.  It only means that this immediate
\((o,1)^r\)-block does not repay the preceding \((o,2)^k\)-debt by
itself.  Later blocks, or an exit to \((o,0)\), may still repay the
accumulated debt.

\subsubsection{Arithmetic form of one alternating block}

Let
\[
r=v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2).
\]
Then there is an integer \(s\), with \(3\nmid s\), such that
\[
5\cdot 2^kq-2=3^rs.
\]
Solving for \(q\),
\[
q=\frac{3^rs+2}{5\cdot 2^k}.
\]
Since
\[
m_0=3^kq-1,
\]
we get
\[
\boxed{
m_0=
\frac{3^k(3^rs+2)}{5\cdot 2^k}-1,
\qquad
3\nmid s.
}
\]
The condition that \(m_0\) be an integer is
\[
\boxed{
5\cdot 2^k\mid 3^rs+2.
}
\]
Equivalently,
\[
3^rs\equiv -2\pmod {5\cdot 2^k}.
\]
Since \(3^r\) is invertible modulo \(5\cdot 2^k\), this determines \(s\)
modulo \(5\cdot 2^k\):
\[
\boxed{
s\equiv -2(3^r)^{-1}\pmod {5\cdot 2^k}.
}
\]

This integer condition is independent of repayment.  It only says that
the block
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r
\]
is arithmetically admissible.  Repayment is the separate inequality
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]

\subsubsection{Examples}

The following table gives representative values.  We choose \(q\) so
that the exit-to-\((o,1)\) condition
\[
2^kq\equiv 1\pmod 3
\]
is satisfied.  Then we compute
\[
r=v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2).
\]
The column ``Repays?'' refers to the exact leading-multiplier condition
\[
r>0.414k.
\]

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c|c}
k & q & m_0=3^kq-1 & 5\cdot 2^kq-2
& r=v_3(\cdot) & \text{Repays?}\\
\hline
1 & 2 & 5 & 18 & 2 & \text{yes}\\
2 & 1 & 8 & 18 & 2 & \text{yes}\\
3 & 2 & 53 & 78 & 1 & \text{no}\\
4 & 1 & 80 & 78 & 1 & \text{no}\\
5 & 2 & 485 & 318 & 1 & \text{no}\\
6 & 1 & 728 & 318 & 1 & \text{no}\\
7 & 2 & 4373 & 1278 & 2 & \text{no}\\
8 & 1 & 6560 & 1278 & 2 & \text{no}\\
9 & 5 & 98414 & 12798 & 4 & \text{yes}\\
10 & 1 & 59048 & 5118 & 1 & \text{no}\\
11 & 8 & 1417175 & 81918 & 3 & \text{no}\\
12 & 4 & 2125763 & 81918 & 3 & \text{no}
\end{array}
\]

These examples show that
\[
r=v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2)
\]
can be much smaller than \(k/2\).  For example, when
\[
k=10,\qquad q=1,
\]
we have
\[
5\cdot 2^{10}\cdot 1-2=5118=3\cdot 1706,
\]
so
\[
r=1.
\]
Thus a long \((o,2)\)-run can be followed by a short \((o,1)\)-run.
Local repayment is therefore not automatic.

On the other hand, when
\[
k=9,\qquad q=5,
\]
we get
\[
5\cdot 2^9\cdot 5-2=12798=3^4\cdot 158,
\]
so
\[
r=4.
\]
Since
\[
0.414\cdot 9\approx 3.726,
\]
this block repays its debt.

\subsubsection{Indefinite alternating runs and cumulative debt}

A single block need not repay locally.  Therefore we pass to cumulative
debt.

Consider an alternating sequence
\[
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
(o,2)^{k_2}(o,1)^{r_2}
\cdots
(o,2)^{k_N}(o,1)^{r_N}.
\]
Define
\[
K_N=\sum_{i=1}^N k_i,
\qquad
R_N=\sum_{i=1}^N r_i.
\]
The cumulative leading reverse multiplier is
\[
G_N=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{K_N}
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{R_N}.
\]
The cumulative debt is repaid once
\[
G_N>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^{R_N}
>
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{K_N}.
\]
Taking logarithms gives
\[
R_N\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
>
K_N\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right).
\]
Thus cumulative repayment occurs when
\[
\boxed{
R_N>
K_N\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414K_N.
}
\]
A cleaner sufficient condition is
\[
\boxed{
R_N\ge \frac12K_N.
}
\]

Thus local under-repayment is allowed.  The only dangerous case is
permanent cumulative under-repayment:
\[
\boxed{
R_N\le 0.414K_N
\quad\text{for every }N.
}
\]

An indefinite alternating sequence must contain infinitely many
\((o,1)\)-instances, since otherwise it would eventually become an
infinite \((o,2)\)-run, which has already been ruled out.  However,
infinitely many \((o,1)\)-instances are not enough by themselves.  The
\((o,1)\)-instances must occur often enough relative to the
\((o,2)\)-instances to clear the cumulative debt.

\subsubsection{Block-to-block recurrence}

Suppose a short \((o,1)\)-run exits back to \((o,2)\).  Then we can
write a recurrence for the next possible exceptional block.

Starting from
\[
m_0=3^kq-1,
\qquad
3\nmid q,
\]
we have
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]
Let
\[
5\cdot 2^kq-2=3^rs,
\qquad
3\nmid s.
\]
After the \((o,1)^r\)-run,
\[
5m_r+3=8^rs,
\]
so
\[
m_r=\frac{8^rs-3}{5}.
\]
If the run exits from \((o,1)\) back to \((o,2)\), then the next
\((o,2)\) input index is
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}=\frac{8m_r+2}{3}.
\]
Substituting the formula for \(m_r\), we get
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8\left(\frac{8^rs-3}{5}\right)+2}{3}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\boxed{
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s-14}{15}.
}
\]
The next exceptional \((o,2)\)-run length is
\[
k_{\mathrm{next}}
=
v_3(m_{\mathrm{next}}+1).
\]
Since
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}+1
=
\frac{8^{r+1}s+1}{15},
\]
we obtain
\[
\boxed{
k_{\mathrm{next}}
=
v_3(8^{r+1}s+1)-1.
}
\]

Thus repeated short repayment is not arbitrary.  It requires the
recurrence
\[
s_i=\frac{5\cdot 2^{k_i}q_i-2}{3^{r_i}},
\]
\[
q_{i+1}
=
\frac{8^{r_i+1}s_i+1}{15\cdot 3^{k_{i+1}}},
\]
with
\[
3\nmid q_i,
\qquad
3\nmid s_i.
\]
An infinite permanently under-repaying alternation would therefore impose
an infinite nested sequence of \(3\)-adic congruence conditions on the
original starting value.

\subsubsection{Current remaining obstruction}

The preceding analysis reduces the remaining problem to the following
precise obstruction:

\begin{center}
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.88\textwidth}
\centering
Can there exist an infinite admissible alternation
\[
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
(o,2)^{k_2}(o,1)^{r_2}\cdots ?
\]
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}
such that
\[
\boxed{
R_N\le 0.414K_N
\quad\text{for every }N?
}
\]

If no such indefinitely under-repaying alternation exists, then some
finite prefix satisfies
\[
R_N>0.414K_N,
\]
and the cumulative debt is cleared.  At that point the corresponding
reverse segment has net leading growth, giving the desired forward
descent over that segment.

Thus the next task is to rule out permanent cumulative under-repayment,
either by a direct valuation argument, by identifying an inadmissible
\(3\)-adic limiting object, or by finding a corrected Lyapunov function
that decreases along every admissible dangerous transition.

\subsubsection{Modular depth as a proxy for debt}
\label{subsubsec:modular-depth-debt-proxy}

For a block of the form
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r,
\]
the starting index \(m_0\) is forced into a single residue class modulo
\[
3^{k+r}.
\]
More precisely,
\[
m_0\equiv A(k,r)\pmod {3^{k+r}},
\]
where
\[
A(k,r)
=
3^k\left(2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}\bmod 3^r\right)-1.
\]
Thus the block fixes
\[
\boxed{
L=k+r
}
\]
base-\(3\) digits of \(m_0\).  We call \(L\) the \emph{modular depth} of
the block.

On the other hand, the same block has leading reverse multiplier
\[
G(k,r)
=
\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^k
\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)^r.
\]
Equivalently, the logarithmic debt change is
\[
\Delta D(k,r)
=
k\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
r\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
\]
Thus \(k\) contributes debt, while \(r\) contributes repayment.

Since
\[
L=k+r,
\]
we may write
\[
\rho=\frac{r}{k+r}.
\]
Then
\[
k=(1-\rho)L,
\qquad
r=\rho L.
\]
Substituting into the debt change gives
\[
\Delta D
=
L\left[
(1-\rho)\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
\rho\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
\right].
\]
Therefore the debt change per fixed ternary digit is
\[
\boxed{
\frac{\Delta D}{L}
=
(1-\rho)\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
\rho\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
}
\]

The block repays its debt precisely when
\[
\Delta D<0.
\]
This is equivalent to
\[
(1-\rho)\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
<
\rho\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
\]
Rearranging,
\[
\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
<
\rho\left[
\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
+
\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
\right].
\]
Hence repayment occurs when
\[
\boxed{
\rho>
\frac{
\log(3/2)
}{
\log(3/2)+\log(8/3)
}.
}
\]
Numerically,
\[
\frac{
\log(3/2)
}{
\log(3/2)+\log(8/3)
}
\approx 0.293.
\]
Thus:
\begin{center}
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\centering
A block repays its debt if more than about 29.3\% of its modular depth
comes from \((o,1)\)-steps.
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}

Equivalently, since
\[
\rho=\frac{r}{k+r},
\]
this condition is the same as
\[
\frac{r}{k}> \frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414.
\]
Thus the two forms of the repayment condition agree:
\[
\boxed{
r>0.414k
}
\]
or, equivalently,
\[
\boxed{
\frac{r}{k+r}>0.293.
}
\]

This gives a useful interpretation.  The modular depth
\[
L=k+r
\]
measures how many ternary digits of the starting index \(m_0\) are fixed
by the prescribed block, while
\[
\Delta D
=
k\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
r\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right)
\]
measures whether those fixed digits correspond to net debt or net
repayment.

Thus modular depth can serve as a proxy for debt, provided we keep track
of how much of the depth comes from \((o,2)\)-steps and how much comes
from \((o,1)\)-steps.  The total depth alone is not enough; the important
quantity is the proportion
\[
\rho=\frac{r}{k+r}.
\]

For an alternating prefix
\[
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
(o,2)^{k_2}(o,1)^{r_2}
\cdots
(o,2)^{k_N}(o,1)^{r_N},
\]
define
\[
K_N=\sum_{i=1}^{N}k_i,
\qquad
R_N=\sum_{i=1}^{N}r_i,
\qquad
L_N=K_N+R_N.
\]
Then the prefix fixes \(L_N\) ternary digits of \(m_0\), and its
cumulative debt is
\[
D_N
=
K_N\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
R_N\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
\]
Writing
\[
\rho_N=\frac{R_N}{L_N},
\]
we get
\[
\frac{D_N}{L_N}
=
(1-\rho_N)\log\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)
-
\rho_N\log\left(\frac{8}{3}\right).
\]
Therefore cumulative debt clears once
\[
\boxed{
\rho_N>0.293.
}
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\boxed{
R_N>0.414K_N.
}
\]

\paragraph{Example residue table.}

For a block of the form
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r,
\]
the starting index satisfies
\[
m_0\equiv A(k,r)\pmod {3^{k+r}},
\]
where
\[
A(k,r)
=
3^k\left(2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}\bmod 3^r\right)-1.
\]
The following table lists the residue classes for small values of \(k\)
and \(r\).  Each entry should be read as
\[
m_0\equiv A(k,r)\pmod {3^{k+r}}.
\]

\begingroup\scriptsize
\[
\begin{array}{@{}c|c|c|c|c@{}}
k & r=1 & r=2 & r=3 & r=4\\
\hline
0 & 0\pmod 3 & 3\pmod 9 & 21\pmod {27} & 48\pmod {81}\\
1 & 5\pmod 9 & 5\pmod {27} & 32\pmod {81} & 194\pmod {243}\\
2 & 8\pmod {27} & 8\pmod {81} & 170\pmod {243} & 656\pmod {729}\\
3 & 53\pmod {81} & 134\pmod {243} & 620\pmod {729} & 2078\pmod {2187}\\
4 & 80\pmod {243} & 566\pmod {729} & 2024\pmod {2187} & 6398\pmod {6561}\\
5 & 485\pmod {729} & 1943\pmod {2187} & 6317\pmod {6561} & 19439\pmod {19683}
\end{array}
\]
\endgroup

For example, the entry
\[
k=2,\qquad r=3
\]
says that the prescribed block
\[
(o,2)^2(o,1)^3
\]
forces
\[
m_0\equiv 170\pmod {243}.
\]
This refines the earlier condition for
\[
(o,2)^2\to(o,1),
\]
namely
\[
m_0\equiv 8\pmod {27},
\]
because
\[
170\equiv 8\pmod {27}.
\]

Thus the table illustrates the nested-residue structure: adding an
\((o,1)^r\)-run does not replace the earlier \((o,2)^k\)-condition; it
lifts it to a narrower congruence class modulo \(3^{k+r}\).

This reformulates the remaining obstruction in modular terms.  An
indefinite alternating pattern can remain dangerous only if the
proportion of newly fixed ternary digits contributed by \((o,1)\)-runs
never exceeds approximately \(29.3\%\).  If at some finite stage the
\((o,1)\)-share of the modular depth exceeds this threshold, the
cumulative debt is cleared.

\subsubsection{Nested residues, lift coefficients, and the finite-positive obstruction}
\label{subsubsec:nested-residues-lift-coefficients}

We now record the modular form of the alternating obstruction.  The aim
is to understand what a starting index \(m_0\) would have to look like if
it supported a long, or even infinite, alternating segment
\[
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
(o,2)^{k_2}(o,1)^{r_2}
\cdots .
\]

\paragraph{One alternating block.}

For a single block
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r,
\]
we begin with an exact \((o,2)^k\)-run:
\[
m_0+1=3^k q,
\qquad 3\nmid q.
\]
Thus
\[
m_0=3^kq-1.
\]
After the \((o,2)^k\)-run,
\[
m_k=2^kq-1.
\]
The following \((o,1)^r\)-run is measured by
\[
r=v_3(5m_k+3).
\]
Substituting \(m_k=2^kq-1\), we obtain
\[
r=v_3(5\cdot 2^kq-2).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
3^r\mid 5\cdot 2^kq-2.
\]
Hence
\[
5\cdot 2^kq\equiv 2\pmod {3^r}.
\]
Since \(5\cdot 2^k\) is invertible modulo \(3^r\),
\[
q\equiv 2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}\pmod {3^r}.
\]
Therefore
\[
m_0=3^kq-1
\]
is forced into a single residue class modulo \(3^{k+r}\):
\[
\boxed{
m_0\equiv A(k,r)\pmod {3^{k+r}},
}
\]
where
\[
\boxed{
A(k,r)
=
3^k\left(2(5\cdot 2^k)^{-1}\bmod 3^r\right)-1.
}
\]

Thus a block \((o,2)^k(o,1)^r\) fixes
\[
L=k+r
\]
ternary digits of \(m_0\).  We call \(L\) the modular depth of the block.

\paragraph{Residue table for one block.}

The following table lists \(A(k,r)\) for small values of \(k\) and \(r\).
Each entry is to be read as
\[
m_0\equiv A(k,r)\pmod {3^{k+r}}.
\]

\begingroup\scriptsize
\[
\begin{array}{@{}c|c|c|c|c@{}}
k & r=1 & r=2 & r=3 & r=4\\
\hline
0 & 0\pmod 3 & 3\pmod 9 & 21\pmod {27} & 48\pmod {81}\\
1 & 5\pmod 9 & 5\pmod {27} & 32\pmod {81} & 194\pmod {243}\\
2 & 8\pmod {27} & 8\pmod {81} & 170\pmod {243} & 656\pmod {729}\\
3 & 53\pmod {81} & 134\pmod {243} & 620\pmod {729} & 2078\pmod {2187}\\
4 & 80\pmod {243} & 566\pmod {729} & 2024\pmod {2187} & 6398\pmod {6561}\\
5 & 485\pmod {729} & 1943\pmod {2187} & 6317\pmod {6561} & 19439\pmod {19683}
\end{array}
\]
\endgroup

For example,
\[
k=2,\qquad r=3
\]
gives
\[
m_0\equiv 170\pmod {243}.
\]
This refines the earlier exit condition
\[
(o,2)^2\to(o,1),
\]
which required
\[
m_0\equiv 8\pmod {27},
\]
because
\[
170\equiv 8\pmod {27}.
\]

Thus adding an \((o,1)^r\)-run does not replace the previous
\((o,2)^k\)-condition.  It lifts that condition to a narrower residue
class modulo a higher power of \(3\).

\paragraph{Debt and modular depth.}

The leading reverse multiplier of the block
\[
(o,2)^k(o,1)^r
\]
is
\[
G(k,r)
=
\left(\frac23\right)^k
\left(\frac83\right)^r.
\]
Thus the block repays its \((o,2)\)-debt precisely when
\[
G(k,r)>1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\left(\frac83\right)^r>\left(\frac32\right)^k.
\]
Clearing denominators gives the exact integer criterion
\[
\boxed{
2^{3r+k}>3^{r+k}.
}
\]
Thus the block repays debt if and only if
\[
2^{3r+k}-3^{r+k}>0.
\]
It remains under-repaying if
\[
2^{3r+k}-3^{r+k}<0.
\]

In logarithmic form, repayment is equivalent to
\[
r>
k\frac{\log(3/2)}{\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.414k.
\]
Equivalently, if
\[
L=k+r,
\qquad
\rho=\frac{r}{L},
\]
then repayment occurs when
\[
\rho>
\frac{\log(3/2)}
{\log(3/2)+\log(8/3)}
\approx 0.293.
\]
So a block repays if more than about \(29.3\%\) of its fixed ternary
digits come from the \((o,1)\)-recovery part.

\paragraph{Two-block prefixes and lift coefficients.}

Now consider a two-block prefix
\[
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
(o,2)^{k_2}(o,1)^{r_2}.
\]
Let
\[
L_1=k_1+r_1,
\qquad
L_2=k_1+r_1+k_2+r_2.
\]
The first block gives
\[
m_0\equiv A_1\pmod {3^{L_1}}.
\]
The two-block prefix gives
\[
m_0\equiv A_2\pmod {3^{L_2}}.
\]
Since the second congruence refines the first, we have
\[
A_2\equiv A_1\pmod {3^{L_1}}.
\]
Therefore there is an integer \(c_1\) such that
\[
\boxed{
A_2=A_1+3^{L_1}c_1.
}
\]
We call \(c_1\) the lift coefficient.  It records the residue lift modulo a higher power of \(3\) forced by the
second block.

More generally, after \(N\) alternating blocks we have
\[
m_0\equiv A_N\pmod {3^{L_N}},
\]
where
\[
L_N=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(k_i+r_i).
\]
The next block gives
\[
m_0\equiv A_{N+1}\pmod {3^{L_{N+1}}},
\]
and hence
\[
\boxed{
A_{N+1}=A_N+3^{L_N}c_N.
}
\]
Thus \(c_N\) records the residue lift modulo a higher power of \(3\)
imposed by the next alternating block.

\paragraph{Two-block table.}

The following table illustrates the two-block lift.  The cumulative debt
is tested exactly using
\[
2^{3R+K}>3^{R+K},
\]
where
\[
K=k_1+k_2,
\qquad
R=r_1+r_2.
\]

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c|c|c}
(k_1,r_1) & (k_2,r_2) & A_1 \bmod 3^{L_1}
& A_2 \bmod 3^{L_2} & c_1 & (K,R) & \text{Debt}\\
\hline
(1,1) & (2,4) & 5 \bmod 9
& 2291 \bmod 6561 & 254 & (3,5) & \text{repaid}\\
(1,2) & (2,2) & 5 \bmod 27
& 1679 \bmod 2187 & 62 & (3,4) & \text{repaid}\\
(1,3) & (1,2) & 32 \bmod 81
& 1085 \bmod 2187 & 13 & (2,5) & \text{repaid}\\
(2,1) & (5,1) & 8 \bmod 27
& 11456 \bmod 19683 & 424 & (7,2) & \text{unpaid}\\
(2,2) & (2,2) & 8 \bmod 81
& 2519 \bmod 6561 & 31 & (4,4) & \text{repaid}\\
(2,2) & (3,1) & 8 \bmod 81
& 4706 \bmod 6561 & 58 & (5,3) & \text{repaid}\\
(3,2) & (5,1) & 134 \bmod 243
& 56267 \bmod 177147 & 231 & (8,3) & \text{unpaid}\\
(4,2) & (5,1) & 566 \bmod 729
& 84401 \bmod 531441 & 115 & (9,3) & \text{unpaid}\\
(5,1) & (1,1) & 485 \bmod 729
& 3401 \bmod 6561 & 4 & (6,2) & \text{unpaid}
\end{array}
\]

This table shows that the lift coefficient \(c_1\) is usually nonzero.
In those cases, the next block forces a nonzero residue lift modulo a
higher power of \(3\) for \(m_0\).

\paragraph{The meaning of \(c_N=0\).}

The condition
\[
c_N=0
\]
means
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N.
\]
Thus the ordinary residue value does not change, although the modulus
does become deeper:
\[
m_0\equiv A_N\pmod {3^{L_N}}
\]
is refined to
\[
m_0\equiv A_N\pmod {3^{L_{N+1}}},
\qquad
L_{N+1}>L_N.
\]
Equivalently, the next block imposes only the zero lift modulo the next
higher power of \(3\).

This is important for distinguishing a genuinely \(3\)-adic infinite
candidate from a finite positive integer.  Suppose an infinite
alternating sequence is realized by a fixed positive integer \(M\).  Then
for every \(N\),
\[
M\equiv A_N\pmod {3^{L_N}}.
\]
Since \(L_N\to\infty\), eventually
\[
3^{L_N}>M.
\]
At that point, \(A_N\), as the least nonnegative representative modulo
\(3^{L_N}\), must equal \(M\) itself:
\[
A_N=M.
\]
Therefore all later successful refinements must satisfy
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N=M.
\]
But since
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N+3^{L_N}c_N,
\]
we get
\[
\boxed{
c_N=0
}
\]
for all sufficiently large \(N\).

Thus we have proved:

\begin{center}
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\centering
If a finite positive integer supports an infinite alternating segment,
then \(c_N=0\) eventually.
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}

This does not mean that \(c_N=0\) alone guarantees an infinite orbit.
Rather, it is a necessary condition for an infinite alternating segment
to be carried by an ordinary positive integer.

\paragraph{Example 1: zero lift with debt already repaid.}

Consider
\[
m_0=170.
\]
We have
\[
m_0+1=171=3^2\cdot 19,
\]
so
\[
k_1=2,\qquad q_1=19.
\]
After the \((o,2)^2\)-run,
\[
m_\ast=2^2q_1-1=4\cdot 19-1=75.
\]
The following \((o,1)\)-run length is
\[
r_1=v_3(5m_\ast+3).
\]
Since
\[
5m_\ast+3=5\cdot 75+3=378=3^3\cdot 14,
\]
we get
\[
r_1=3,
\qquad
s_1=14.
\]
Thus the first block is
\[
(o,2)^2(o,1)^3.
\]
The residue condition is
\[
m_0\equiv 170\pmod {243}.
\]

After the \((o,1)^3\)-run,
\[
m_{k+r}
=
\frac{8^3s_1-3}{5}
=
\frac{512\cdot 14-3}{5}
=
1433.
\]
If this exits back to \((o,2)\), the next \((o,2)\)-input is
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}
=
\frac{8m_{k+r}+2}{3}
=
\frac{8\cdot 1433+2}{3}
=
3822.
\]
Then
\[
m_{\mathrm{next}}+1=3823,
\]
which is not divisible by \(3\).  Hence
\[
k_2=0.
\]
The next \((o,1)\)-length is
\[
r_2=v_3(5\cdot 3823-2).
\]
Since
\[
5\cdot 3823-2=19113=3\cdot 6371,
\qquad 3\nmid 6371,
\]
we have
\[
r_2=1.
\]
Thus \(m_0=170\) realizes the longer prefix
\[
(o,2)^2(o,1)^3(o,2)^0(o,1)^1.
\]

The first modular depth is
\[
L_1=2+3=5,
\]
so
\[
3^{L_1}=243.
\]
The second modular depth is
\[
L_2=2+3+0+1=6,
\]
so
\[
3^{L_2}=729.
\]
The residue remains
\[
A_1=A_2=170.
\]
Therefore
\[
A_2=A_1+3^{L_1}c_1
\]
gives
\[
170=170+243c_1,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
c_1=0.
}
\]

In this example, however, the debt is already repaid.  For the first
block alone,
\[
K=2,\qquad R=3.
\]
The exact debt test gives
\[
2^{3R+K}=2^{11}=2048,
\]
while
\[
3^{R+K}=3^5=243.
\]
Thus
\[
2^{11}>3^5,
\]
so the debt is repaid already by
\[
(o,2)^2(o,1)^3.
\]

\paragraph{Example 2: temporary zero-lift behavior during an unpaid prefix.}

A finite unpaid prefix can also occur.  Consider
\[
m_0=27026.
\]
This value begins with the alternating blocks
\[
(o,2)^3(o,1)^1
(o,2)^4(o,1)^1
(o,2)^2(o,1)^1.
\]
The cumulative totals are
\[
K=3+4+2=9,
\qquad
R=1+1+1=3.
\]
The exact debt test is
\[
2^{3R+K}>3^{R+K}.
\]
Here
\[
2^{3(3)+9}=2^{18}=262144,
\]
while
\[
3^{3+9}=3^{12}=531441.
\]
Hence
\[
2^{18}<3^{12},
\]
so the cumulative debt remains unpaid after these three alternating
blocks.

The modular depths are
\[
L_1=4,
\qquad
L_2=9,
\qquad
L_3=12.
\]
The corresponding residues satisfy
\[
27026\equiv 53\pmod {81},
\]
\[
27026\equiv 7343\pmod {19683},
\]
and
\[
27026\equiv 27026\pmod {531441}.
\]
At the third stage the modulus already exceeds \(m_0\), so the least
nonnegative residue is literally
\[
A_3=27026.
\]
If this same \(m_0\) continued to satisfy deeper prefixes indefinitely,
then all later residues would have to remain equal to \(27026\), and all
later lift coefficients would be zero.

However, in this example the alternating pattern does not continue
indefinitely; it exits to the \((o,0)\) channel after the next attempted
continuation.  Thus the example shows that unpaid finite prefixes can
occur, and that residue stabilization can appear temporarily, but it
does not produce an infinite under-repaying alternating orbit.

The apparent obstruction is an infinite alternating exceptional segment.
The next sections show that this obstruction cannot occur for a finite
positive segment-start: either the nested residue object is only
\(3\)-adic, or the residues stabilize and the segment is exhausted.

The examples computed above illustrate both possibilities.  For
\(m_0=170\), one obtains a zero lift \(c_N=0\), but the debt has already
been repaid.  For \(m_0=27026\), one obtains a finite under-repaying
prefix; however, the modulus eventually exceeds the finite value itself,
so the residue stabilizes to the actual integer:
\[
A_N=m_0,
\qquad
y_N=0.
\]
These examples show the same mechanism that appears in the general proof.


\subsubsection{Stabilization and exhaustion}
\label{subsubsec:stabilization-and-exhaustion-short}

The preceding residue construction gives, for each finite prefix of a
compensated exceptional segment,
\[
m_0=A_N+3^{L_N}y_N,
\qquad
A_{N+1}=A_N+3^{L_N}c_N.
\]
Here \(L_N\) is the cumulative residue depth, and \(c_N\) records the
residue lift modulo a higher power of \(3\) imposed by the next block.

If the compensated exceptional segment were extendable indefinitely, then
\[
L_N\to\infty.
\]
For a finite positive segment-start \(m_0=M\), eventually
\[
3^{L_N}>M.
\]
Since \(0\leq A_N<3^{L_N}\), the representation
\[
M=A_N+3^{L_N}y_N
\]
forces
\[
A_N=M,
\qquad
 y_N=0.
\]
All later lifts then satisfy
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N=M,
\qquad
c_N=0.
\]
Thus the residue construction stabilizes and collapses to the identity
\[
m_0=m_0.
\]
%At that point the current residue-building segment has exhausted its
%ability to impose further residue information on its segment-start.  If
%the residues do not stabilize, then the limiting object exists only in the
%\(3\)-adic sense and is not an ordinary finite positive integer.  Hence an
%attempted infinite compensated exceptional segment either leaves the
%positive integer domain or stabilizes and is exhausted.

\subsubsection{Conclusion: no infinite contiguous exceptional segment}
\label{subsubsec:no-infinite-contiguous-exceptional-segment}

We can now state the main conclusion of the preceding analysis.

An exceptional segment is a contiguous alternating episode of the form
\[
(o,2)^{k_0}(o,1)^{r_0}
(o,2)^{k_1}(o,1)^{r_1}
\cdots .
\]
For a finite prefix of such a segment, the original segment-start \(m_0\)
is forced into a residue class
\[
m_0\equiv A_N \pmod {3^{L_N}},
\]
where
\[
L_N
=
\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}(k_i+r_i).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
m_0=A_N+3^{L_N}y_N.
\]

Appending another block gives the lift relation
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N+3^{L_N}c_N.
\]
Thus \(c_N\) measures whether the next block contributes a residue lift modulo a higher power of \(3\) to the original segment-start \(m_0\).

Now suppose that a single contiguous exceptional segment continued
indefinitely from a finite positive value \(m_0=M\).  Then
\[
L_N\to\infty.
\]
Hence, for all sufficiently large \(N\),
\[
3^{L_N}>M.
\]
Since \(A_N\) is the least nonnegative residue modulo \(3^{L_N}\),
\[
0\leq A_N<3^{L_N}.
\]
Therefore the representation
\[
M=A_N+3^{L_N}y_N
\]
forces
\[
A_N=M
\]
and
\[
y_N=0.
\]
Consequently, the residue system stabilizes.

Moreover, for all later refinements,
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N=M.
\]
Since
\[
A_{N+1}=A_N+3^{L_N}c_N,
\]
we also obtain
\[
c_N=0
\]
for all sufficiently large \(N\).

Thus an attempted infinite exceptional segment has only two possible
interpretations.

First, if the residues \(A_N\) do not stabilize, then the limiting object
is only a \(3\)-adic integer:
\[
m_\infty=\lim_{N\to\infty}A_N
\quad\text{in }\mathbb Z_3.
\]
This does not give an ordinary finite positive starting value.

Second, if the residues do stabilize, then
\[
A_N=m_0,
\qquad
y_N=0,
\qquad
c_N=0.
\]
At that point the residue condition has collapsed to the identity
\[
m_0=m_0.
\]
The current exceptional segment has exhausted its ability to impose
further residue information on \(m_0\).  Hence it has ended as a
residue-building segment.


Furthermore, since the run has exact length \(k\), we have written
\[
m_0+1=3^kq,
\qquad
3\nmid q.
\]
After \(k\) consecutive \((o,2)\)-steps, we found
\[
m_k+1=2^kq.
\]
Because
\[
3\nmid 2^k
\]
and
\[
3\nmid q,
\]
it follows that
\[
3\nmid m_k+1.
\]
Equivalently,
\[
\nu_3(m_k+1)=0.
\]

This is precisely the exit condition.  The next \((o,2)\)-self-loop
would require
\[
m_k\equiv -1\pmod 3,
\]
or equivalently,
\[
3\mid m_k+1.
\]
But the exact-length assumption gives
\[
\nu_3(m_k+1)=0,
\]
so this condition fails.  Therefore the pure \((o,2)\)-run stops exactly
after \(k\) steps.


\begin{proposition}[Stabilization exhausts a residue-building segment]
\label{prop:stabilization-exhausts-residue-building-segment}
A contiguous exceptional \((o,2)/(o,1)\)-segment beginning from a finite
positive state cannot continue indefinitely as a residue-building segment
once its residue data have stabilized.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The preceding stabilization argument shows that an attempted infinite
segment has only two possible interpretations.  If the residues \(A_N\)
do not stabilize, the limiting object is only \(3\)-adic and is not a
finite positive integer.  If the residues do stabilize, then
\[
A_N=m_0,\qquad y_N=0,\qquad c_N=0
\]
from some finite stage onward, so the residue condition collapses to
\[
m_0=m_0.
\]
Thus the current exceptional segment has exhausted its ability to impose
further residue information on \(m_0\), and cannot continue indefinitely
as one residue-building segment.

Since \(A_N\) is already the finite integer \(m_0\) itself, A further genuine dangerous continuation will be shown below to require
a nonzero residue lift modulo a higher power of \(3\).  That would require
\[
c_N\neq 0.
\]
But stabilization gives \(c_N=0\) for all sufficiently large \(N\).  Hence
the current residue-building segment has exhausted its ability to impose
further residue information on its segment-start.

\end{proof}

Therefore, once its residue data have stabilized, a single contiguous
exceptional segment cannot continue indefinitely as the same
residue-building process.

We may summarize the conclusion as follows:
\begin{center}
\fbox{\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\centering
Once the residue data stabilize, the current residue-building
segment has exhausted its ability to impose new constraints on $m_0$.
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}

This does not prevent the orbit from later entering another exceptional
episode.  However, such a later episode is a new segment, not a
continuation of the previous one.  By the same argument, the new
exceptional segment is finite as well.

Thus the stabilization mechanism shows how a residue-building exceptional
segment exhausts its ability to impose new constraints on a finite
positive starting value.  The formal exclusion of infinite compensated
exceptional segments will be completed below, after proving that genuine
dangerous continuation requires a nonzero residue lift modulo a higher
power of \(3\).


\paragraph{Disposal of the \(\omega_1\)-exception.}
The drift table contains two \(p=0\), \(\alpha=1\) rows:
\[
\omega_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Omega_2.
\]
Both have one-step leading reverse multiplier
\[
\frac{2}{3},
\]
and hence either one may create a single unit of local debt.  However,
they differ in an essential structural way.

The row \(\Omega_2\) has type \(oo\).  Thus it remains in the odd family,
and in the exceptional router class it can produce a same-symbol return
\[
(o,2)\longrightarrow(o,2).
\]
This is why \(\Omega_2\) can generate long exceptional blocks
\[
(o,2)^k,
\]
whose accumulated leading debt is
\[
\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^k.
\]

By contrast, the row \(\omega_1\) has type \(oe\).  It starts in the
state \((o,1)\), but its output lies in the \(e\)-family:
\[
(o,1)\xrightarrow{\omega_1}(e,j')
\]
for some output router \(j'\in\{0,1,2\}\).  Therefore a \(\omega_1\)-step
cannot be followed by another \(\omega_1\)-step without first returning
